## Chapter 3

## **Measuring and Assessing Program Structure: Intended Performance**

### **Overview**

This chapter presents instruments and methods to measure and assess the design of the academic programs to follow established standards and optimize learning. Functional design creates many institutional performance issues and the credible and trustworthy measurements of the performance of each aspect of design informs the processes of institutional improvement. These instruments aim to collect authentic data with which to monitor the design of the components, processes, and procedures (Institutional Vision) that deliver the institutional mission. The resulting data describe the attributes of functional design and measure their contribution to institutional performance and effectiveness.

This assessment process measures three dimensions of program design: the design of the curriculum and courses, the delivery of the program learning, and the viability of a program to perform in the external environment (Ali, 2018). Together, these assessments measure the ability of the program design structures to achieve the mission (Casarejos, Gustavson, & Frota, 2017).

### **Measuring Curriculum**

The process of delivering the institutional mission, vision, and values begins with the design of the curricula and the courses that deliver those curricula (Mouritsen, 1986). The curriculum and the courses engineer the instructional experience to allow learners to achieve the learning effectively and efficiently the institution advertises, and each student seeks (Rubenstein & Ridgley, 2017). A curriculum contributes two influences on the success of the students: a prescribed path to follow and “guardrails” to prevent failure. From an instructional systems design perspective, the curriculum creates the drivers and the constraints for the learning process. The availability of trustworthy and credible data to describe the attributes of the curriculum creates the opportunity to understand how the attributes of curriculum design contribute to student success, persistence, retention, and other meaningful indicators of instructional performance (Duque, 2016; Holder, 2007; Macfadyen & Dawson, 2012). In this assessment system, the purpose of the curriculum design is to promote and enhance the learning needs of the students enrolled in a program and the institution (Barnett, Coate, & Society for Research into Higher Education, 2005; Curry & Wergin, 1993, 1997; Diamond, 2008) and includes any discrete unit of learning: a program, specialization, major, minor, general education, etc.

While there is limited research on the performance of the design attributes of curriculum in higher education, there is literature that articulates a theoretical series of attributes that presume to organize the learning in a manner that creates an effective learning experience (Diamond, 2008; Ewell, 1997; Ferren, 1997; Leow, Neo, & Hew, 2016; Marsh & Willis, 2003). These attributes form a conceptual framework to measure and differentiate curricular design and measure its ability to enable the learning of the students within an institution. In this measurement process, the comparison of the attributes of effective curriculum design with an articulated standard constitutes a direct measurement of a curriculum’s coherence and contribution to institutional performance.

The structure of a curriculum is the primary consideration in the learners’ ability to engage with the intended content and learning processes (Rubenstein & Ridgley, 2017). The student’s ability to grasp and process the disciplinary content in a manner that makes intellectual sense to them is the curricular attribute that makes learning engagement possible. Hence, measurements of the design attributes of the curriculum are a critical consideration in the assessment of student engagement. The measurement data monitor the performance of the curriculum at the transactional level where the learners interact with and “experience” the learning the institution intends. Additionally, correlations between the design of the curriculum and the performance of other functions reveal the synergies of the instructional process for the institution (Sullivan & Wilds, 2001).

An instrument is presented, which uses criteria extracted from the research literature to measure the “qualities” of a curriculum (Diamond, 2008; Ewell, 1997; Ferren, 1997; Marsh & Willis, 2003) based on the following standards.

Externally valid content. This standard describes the consistency of the content of the curriculum with expectations of the external environment. These expectations, published in the literature in the discipline, professional employers, licensing boards, or accrediting agencies (Brown, 2017), build a conceptual framework for the curriculum. This dimension determines the relevancy of curriculum content, which promotes learning engagement through the generation of immediacy for the learners. Externally valid content represents the organization of the discipline as conceived by the collective intelligence of the professionals within each discipline and is more likely to be accessible to a diverse population of learners since a diverse population of professionals, which is theoretically comparable to the student population, created the content. Externally valid curriculum ensures that the learning is relevant to the profession and the discipline and meaningful to a diverse population of students (Thompson, Griffith, & Armon, 2014).

*Coverage*. This standard examines the breadth of the program outcomes that define the program. This dimension determines the ability of the curriculum to deliver a comprehensive conception of the discipline (Mena Report, 2015; Oprandi, 2014) as well as the educational goals of the institution (Oaks, 2015).

*Coherence*. Coherence is a standard often articulated as a criterion for curriculum without definition. The alignment of the curriculum with the psychophysical processes of learning, as articulated in the empirical theories of learning, engineers a coherent learning experience (Marcus, 2001). Designated as a “conceptual framework” (Fortuin, Koppen, & Leemans, 2011), this dimension facilitates the ability of the students to engage with the instructional system. Applying the theories of learning to design the structure of the learning process applies the best evidence available to design the best opportunity to access and learn the material in an efficient manner (Blackmore & Kandiko, 2012; Imam, 2018; Pietarinen, Pyhältö, & Soini, 2017; Pietarinen, Soini, & Pyhältö, 2014). Curriculum coherence is a critical factor in the assessment of performance and improvement in higher education (Hurst, 2016; Kress & Selander, 2012; Pautler, 1989).

*Interconnectedness and Alignment*. This standard determines if the program learning objectives appropriately construct the program outcomes. The most effective learning objectives parse the program outcomes into a constructed learning experience for the students (Ellis, 1998; Gurvitch & Metzler, 2013; Marcus, 2011). This dimension determines the ability of a curriculum to deliver an effective learning experience (Rubenstein & Ridgley, 2017; Swaggerty & Broemmel, 2017).

*Complexity*. This dimension analyzes the curriculum to determine if the depth of learning articulated in the learning objectives is appropriate for the level of learning the curriculum delivers. This dimension determines if the learning objectives create appropriate rigor or depth of learning to produce a comprehensive understanding of the program learning outcomes (Fortuin et al., 2011; Soleimani & Alaee, 2014).

*Outcomes Demonstration*. This standard examines the curriculum to determine if the curriculum presents adequate opportunities for learners to demonstrate program outcomes. This dimension measures the opportunities to demonstrate the program outcomes designed into the curriculum.

Using these criteria as a conceptual framework, an instrument is created to measure the existence of these characteristics in the design of a curriculum. This first tool groups the program learning objectives with the program outcome they construct. [Table 7](#CH003.html#tab7) presents an instrument to measure the structure of a curriculum.

The instrument asks evaluators to examine the curriculum for its demonstration of each criterion, without a subjective evaluation of goodness or badness. The evaluator determines if each program learning objective demonstrates each criterion. The categorical counts report the number of objectives that demonstrate each criterion. The categorical averages indicate the degree of alignment between the design of the curriculum and each criterion (Blouch, Ulrich, & Michenzi, 2016). The resulting data monitor the design of each curriculum and is compared with other functional data to uncover correlations between institutional performance and the attributes of curriculum design (Sullivan & Wilds, 2001).

Table 7:    Curriculum Measurement Tool.

![image](images/C0391003T7.jpg)

![image](images/C0391003T7A.jpg)

*Assumption*: The design of this tool assumes that the design characteristics of a curriculum impact the ability of the students to engage with, learn from, and achieve success in a program of study. This tool collects information that uncovers the correlations between student performance and curricular attributes.

*Target*: Curriculum.

*Unit of Measurement*: Curricular Attributes.

*Administration*: Discipline Prepared Faculty.

### **Measuring Courses**

The courses in an academic program deliver the learning in the curriculum as articulated by the design of the curriculum. The collective design (synergy) of the courses provides another useful dimension to monitor the contribution of the course design to the learning experience. This information describes the interrelationships of the courses (group) as a factor in the learning process.

According to the educational literature (Diamond, 2008; Ewell, 1997; Ferren, 1997; Marsh & Willis, 2003), the following attributes demonstrate the ability of courses to deliver a curriculum efficiently:

*Structure*. Structure refers to the collective ability of the courses to deliver the curriculum effectively and efficiently. While the structure of the curriculum impacts learning engagement, the collective structure of the courses generates the learning experience (Alt, 2015; Borges, Vittori, Nabak, & Lewis, 1998; Clifton, Baldwin, & Wei, 2012; Freeman, Haak, & Wenderoth, 2011).

*Integration*. Integration refers to the distribution of the program learning objectives across courses. There are several ways to do this, which is the decision of the faculty and driven by their conceptualization of the discipline (Bock & Randall, 2014; Islam, Talukder, Taheri, & Blanchard, 2016; Walker & Freeze, 2011). The learning objectives are grouped by outcome, characteristics of the learning, problem-solving, etc., or some other strategy to ensure an optimal learning experience is provided by the courses. An optimal model presents each learning objective the minimum number of occurrences to learn the objective effectively. Addressing a learning objective more than necessary reduces the efficiency of the learning experience (Dirks-Naylor, Wright, & Alston, 2015).

*Sequence*. Sequencing refers to the order in which the learners pursue the courses. The courses are expected to facilitate learning through a well-articulated flow within and across courses, to ensure that the students have the information they need to participate in each course (readiness) (Bock & Randall, 2014; Dirks-Naylor, Wright, & Alston, 2015; Swaggerty & Broemmel, 2017).

*Consistency*. Consistency is a measure of the groupings of the learning objective within courses that is consistent with the structure and thinking systems of the discipline (Gardner, 1999). In other words, the distribution of the learning objectives reflects the structure of each discipline. This consistency between the delivery of the learning and the structure of the discipline optimizes learning and enhances the student learning experience.

An instrument is created to measure the design and organization of courses within a curriculum using these criteria. This instrument is a “Goodness of Fit” (GoF) tool that compares two sets of data or information for congruence. The purpose of this instrument is to measure the organization of the courses as a group to deliver learning.

[Table 8](#CH003.html#tab8) presents an instrument to measure the organization and design of a group of courses within a curriculum. Notice that the unit of measure is the course. The learning objectives are organized by the course groupings so that the aggregated data assess the performance of the group of courses.

Table 8:    Course Structure Measurement.

![image](images/C0391003T8.jpg)

![image](images/C0391003T8A.jpg)

*Assumption*: The design of this tool assumes that the interactions between the courses in a curriculum impact the learning experience of the students. The correlations between these data and other performance data reveal the relationship between the course structure and the student success.

*Target*: Program courses.

*Unit of Measure*ment: Course organization.

*Administration*: Discipline faculty.

This analysis requires an evaluator who is familiar with the structure of the discipline as conceptualized by the program faculty to evaluate the group of courses. The evaluators determine if each group of objectives demonstrates each criterion. The categorical counts report the total number of responses for each criterion and the categorical averages indicate the compliance of the course group with each criterion. These data describe the attributes of the course structure without making a subjective evaluation of goodness or badness. The resulting data are compared with other functional performance data to reveal correlations between institutional performance and the attributes of course design. This strategy allows the performance data, collected from the interactions between the learners and the function, to reveal the actual characteristics of institutional performance. At this phase of the process, the goal is to describe the performance of the institution at the individual (student) level objectively. The assessments demonstrate the performance of each function, and the sensemaking process demonstrates the contribution of each function to the performance of the institution. The resulting understanding of institutional performance informs the planning process for performance improvement.

### **Assessing Curriculum**

The individual transactions data collected in the measurement phase are summarized for each curriculum to assess the design of the curriculum by revealing the degree to which each curriculum aligns with the design criteria. The purpose of this assessment process is to demonstrate the structure of the curriculum as compared with an adopted institutional or discipline-specific model (Porter, 2004).

The measurement data are summarized and aggregated at the multiple institutional levels to demonstrate the structure of the curricula at a program, departmental, or institutional levels.

Using a common data set for all levels of analyses aligns the assessment processes across all levels of analysis. [Table 9](#CH003.html#tab9) presents an assessment tool to summarize (counts) and aggregate (averages) the measurement data to describe the attributes for a specific curriculum.

Table 9:    Curriculum Measurement Program Data Summary.

![image](images/C0391003T9.jpg)

*Target of Assessment*: Single curriculum.

*Unit of Measurement*: Curricular attributes.

*Administration*: Data from measurements.

This level of analysis demonstrates the design attributes of one curriculum. The categorical counts summarize the number of instances that exist within each compliance category and the categorical averages aggregate the measurement data into a demonstration of the entire curriculum structure. In this assessment, the objective is to determine the physical structure of the design of the curriculum. The data collected from multiple curricula are aggregated at the departmental, divisional, or other institutional levels to demonstrate the structure of the curricula for each of these levels of institutional performance. The categorical counts are reported in each of these assessments to facilitate the process of re-aggregating the data into multiple levels of performance.

Table 10:    Curriculum Assessment Institutional Assessment.

![image](images/C0391003T10.jpg)

*Target of Assessment*: Institutional curriculum.

*Unit of Measurement*: Program attributes.

*Administration*: Data from measurements of compliance.

[Table 10](#CH003.html#tab10) provides an example of the aggregation of the measurement data into an assessment of the curriculum design structure at the institutional level.

The categorical counts from the measurement data aggregate the individual program data into a summary of the attributes of the curricula for all programs within an institutional structure. This assessment describes the compliance of all curricula in the institution with the global design criteria. These data monitor the design of the curricula at multiple institutional levels for its compliance with the criteria and are compared with other institutional data to uncover correlations between curricular structure and institutional performance.

### **Course Performance Assessment**

The data collected in the course measurement phase are aggregated in the same ways to describe the functional design of the courses within a curriculum for their compliance with criteria adopted for course design. In this case, it is the effect of the group of courses that are assessed. [Table 11](#CH003.html#tab11) presents a method for aggregating the course measurement data into the assessment criteria and using them to demonstrate the design structure of the courses within a program.

Table 11:    Program Course Structure Data Summary.

![image](images/C0391003T11.jpg)

*Target of Assessment*: Program courses.

*Unit of Measurement*: Single course group.

*Administration*: Data from measurements of attributes.

The counts from the Course Measurements populate this assessment tool, which summarizes (counts) the design of a group of courses and aggregates (averages) the results for each criterion. Note that these data do not evaluate a single courses, which requires a separate assessment process. The resulting data are also compared with other functional data to determine the correlations between the design of courses (group) and institutional performance.

To explore the structure of the courses at an institutional level, the Categorical counts from multiple Program Course Structure Measurements populate an assessment tool, which aggregates the course measurements for a series of programs within the institution or the entire institution. The categorical counts from the program assessments are aggregated into various institutional models. [Table 12](#CH003.html#tab12) presents a method for aggregating this information at the institutional level.

The Categorical Counts for all programs within a division or the institution are aggregated to assess the curricula at the institutional level. The assessments of Curriculum and Courses enable the assessment of the curriculum and the courses as separate phenomena from other functions within the institution.

The aggregated raw data enables the differentiation of the design of various curricula and their courses in multiple summaries in a fully aligned manner. The performance of the assumptions and adopted models of effective curricular design are monitored, demonstrated, and enhanced through this method. In the systematic assessment of institutional effectiveness, these assessments of curricular design demonstrate the design characteristics of curricula and courses and evaluate the structure of each curriculum and its contributions to institutional performance, which is a necessary component of the systematic assessment of institutional performance (Sullivan & Wilds, 2001).

Table 12:    Institutional Course Structure Assessment.

![image](images/C0391003T12.jpg)

*Target of Assessment*: Institutional course design.

*Unit of Measurement*: Program attributes.

Administration: Data from measurements of attributes.

These criteria are global enough to apply to a wide variety of situations and represent a measure of the qualities of a curriculum, instead of “quality.” They provide an opportunity to differentiate curricular design attributes and correlate those attributes with trustworthy and credible measures of student success, which reveals the performance of the design of the curriculum to serve the learning needs of the students.

### **Measuring Program Learning Outcomes**

Program learning outcomes measurement evaluates student work products to measure the extent to which the students’ work demonstrates the program learning outcomes delivered in their academic program. A student portfolio compiled in the Student Learning Outcomes measurement of the instructional system (to be presented later) provides the student work products that demonstrate the Program Learning Outcomes. However, the focus of the program learning outcomes process is the performance of the program design to prepare students to demonstrate the program outcomes. The program learning objectives, which are parsed from the outcomes, construct the criteria used to measure the demonstration of each outcome.

A rubric is constructed to measure the program outcomes, which uses the program learning outcomes as the constructs to be measured and discrete levels of performance to scale and differentiate the measurements. The evaluators use the rubric to measure the student work products for their demonstration of the program outcomes defined by performance criteria.

The rubric presented here is scaled to differentiate levels of performance using defined categories of performance as the criteria. The scale is a nominative scale of qualitative (qualities) categories of performance. The scale uses “no demonstration,” “basic understanding,” “ability to use the understanding,” and “the ability to use the information with mastery or agility” as anchors to delineate the performance levels. Each criterion in this example uses the same scale.

In this measurement process, the evaluator examines the student’s work to determine which level of performance the student has demonstrated. The categories of evaluation are distinct and clearly defined without overlap to ensure trustworthy data.

[Table 13](#CH003.html#tab13) presents a rubric populated with the outcomes of a hypothetical General Psychology program at the undergraduate level.

This instrument is applied to a random sample of student work products. The use of discrete and non-overlapping categories of performance generates sufficient power with a random sample of 25–30 work products. It is not necessary to evaluate all student work products since the focus of this measurement is the program and not the students. The resulting data measure the program learning outcomes performance for each program and compared with other functional data to reveal correlations between institutional performance and program learning outcomes demonstration.

### **Measuring Program Learning Objectives**

A second learning measurement gathers evidence of the design of the program learning objectives to deliver the program outcomes. When combined, the program outcomes data and the program learning objectives data differentiate between the processes of learning (objectives) and the products of the learning (outcomes). These data provide rich information to assess the design of the instructional system and are compared with other functional data to reveal correlations between the instructional delivery (objectives) and the results (outcomes) of that delivery.

Parsing the program learning objectives from the program outcomes creates a synergistic relationship in which the objectives interact to produce outcomes that are greater (synergy) than the sum of the objectives. The goal of this measurement is to collect trustworthy and credible data to directly measure the results of the learning process. In this phase of the institutional assessment process, the outcomes and objectives are measured to demonstrate the efficacy of the instructional system as a functional component of institutional performance.

[Table 14](#CH003.html#tab14) presents a rubric to measure the program learning objectives through the examination of student work products using assignments from the courses. Again, the focus of this measurement is the program’s ability to deliver the learning objectives through the design of the courses.

This instrument measures a random sample of work products totaling 25–30 samples from each course to produce data with adequate power. The construction of the rubric with discrete performance categories produces sufficient power from a minimal sample size. The random sample enables the generalization of the sample data to the total population (Kalkan & Kelecioglu, 2016).

![image](images/C0391003T13A.jpg)

The correlations between these data and other performance data reveal the relationship between the structure of the instructional system and the student success.

*Unit of Measurement*: Program outcomes.

*Administration*: Faculty evaluation of student work.

*The Frequency of Administration*: Once 1 per year.

These instruments provide data to monitor the components of the instructional system to deliver the learning objectives, which in turn produce the program learning outcomes. They provide credible, trustworthy, and authentic measurements of the performance of the program to deliver the learning it identifies in its curriculum. These data are compared with other functional data to reveal correlations between institutional performance and the design of the learning system.

### **Designing an Optimal Rubric**

In the measurement and assessment of program learning outcomes, rubrics guide and structure the decision-making process for the evaluators and facilitate the collection of trustworthy and credible data (Mertler, 2001). This discussion explains the structure of the rubrics used in this process and the systematic thinking that went into these designs. The objective of this discussion is to explain the characteristics of the rubrics proposed in this systematic process.

The research process identified standards to guide the design process and serve as the criteria to evaluate the results (Mertler, 2001; Smit & Birri, 2014; Tierney & Simon, 2004). The initial goal was to create rubrics for the measurement of program learning outcomes demonstration and quickly morphed into a strategy to evaluate student learning products for multiple purposes. The consistency between the designs of the rubrics created at the course, program, and institutional level provides positive and constructive benefits for student and institutional learning (Diamond, 2008; Tierney & Simon, 2004).

The criteria established the following standards for the design of rubrics for program learning measurements.

*Standard 1*: The content of the rubric is the program learning outcomes and the program learning objectives. The outcomes constitute the constructs to measure, and the learning objectives are the criteria for measurement.

*Standard 2*: The rubrics measure the outcomes and objectives through the evaluation of student work products that provide an opportunity for the student to demonstrate the outcome or objective authentically.

*Standard 3*: The learning objectives serve as the performance criteria.

*Standard 4*: The criteria are listed in the first and second vertical columns on the left side of the rubric so that all criteria are easily discernible (and differentiated) to the evaluator.

*Standard 5*: For each criterion, a four-part conceptual framework defines the performance categories demonstrable in the work product using a nominal scale. This scale may follow many models of learning. Included in the model below is an example using Bloom’s Cognitive and Affective Domains, Perry’s Model of Intellectual and Ethical Development, a competency development model, and a performance model (King & Howard-Hamilton, 2000).

The rows of the rubric contain the criteria ordered from left to right. Global descriptors such as acceptable, minimal, exceptional, superior, etc., are not used to label or describe the performance categories as this leads the evaluators to make a subjective evaluation instead of applying the criteria to the assignments. The rubric design passively limits the measurements to the criteria contained in the rubric.

*Standard 6*: The scaling model is translated into the rows to evaluate each criterion. The scale may be the same for all criteria or unique to each criterion. In this example, all performance criteria used the same performance scale.

*Standard 7*: No more than five discrete, non-overlapping performance categories are used for each criterion as the ability to discriminate categories of performance become less precise and more indefinite, which creates a threat to the reliability of the data.

*Standard 8*: The rubric is laid out in a grid pattern, with the low point of the scale on the left moving toward the high point on the right. Most cultures represent time (progress, development, learning) in this manner.

*Standard 9*: The rubric contains no more than seven criteria and four performance categories, although one of these performance categories might be a “non-performance” category, as used in the Program Outcomes/Objectives Assessment Rubrics.

[Table 15](#CH003.html#tab15) demonstrates a generic model of a rubric with multiple scales of measurement.

[Table 16](#CH003.html#tab16) presents a rubric model that applies these standards of rubric construction to the content of the Student Learning Outcomes measurement tool. The performance levels are scaled using a performance-based model.

The rubrics used to evaluate student work products for student learning achievement (discussed in the instructional system assessment process) are the same as those used to evaluate the program for its learning outcomes and learning objective demonstration.

### **Assessing Program Learning**

The assessment of program learning requires the aggregation of two sets of data to demonstrate the outcomes of the instructional system: the program learning outcomes and the program learning objectives. The program outcomes assessment is a summative assessment of the performance of the instructional system to deliver the learning outcomes. The program learning objectives assessment is a formative assessment of the processes that deliver those learning outcomes (the process).

The individual data collected in the measurement process are aggregated and summarized to describe the results (outcomes) of the program learning processes, which the curriculum articulates as learning objectives (courses).

[Table 17](#CH003.html#tab17) presents a tool to aggregate the individual measurements of program outcome performance for a specific program. The learning outcomes in the measurement process define the categories for the aggregation of the data.

The categorical counts summarize each level of demonstration. The categorical averages are calculated from these counts for each level of performance and demonstrate the performance of each program to deliver its program outcomes.

Table 15:    Rubric Model.

![image](images/C0391003T15.jpg)

The data are also aggregated by program to demonstrate the institution’s ability to deliver program outcomes to its students.

[Table 18](#CH003.html#tab18) presents an assessment tool to aggregate the program data into an institutional level assessment of student learning.

The tool reports the categorical counts for each program for each level of performance. The categorical averages calculated from these counts demonstrate the performance of each program to deliver its program learning outcomes to its students. These data are also correlated with other functional performance data to reveal relationships between outcome performance and other attributes of the instructional system.

Table 16:    Program Learning Outcomes Measurement Rubric Model.

![image](images/C0391003T16.jpg)

![image](images/C0391003T16A.jpg)

### **Program Learning Objectives Assessment**

The program learning objectives measure the individual performance of the objectives in the courses using the Program Learning Objectives Measurement process. These measurements are summarized and aggregated into assessments to demonstrate the performance of the courses to deliver the learning objectives for each program, which construct the program outcomes. Parsing the learning objectives from the learning outcomes creates a constructed model of curriculum and learning experience (Alt, 2015; Brandon & All, 2010; Henson, 2012; Ültanir & Ültanir, 2010). In this model of curriculum, the learning objectives produce outcomes that are greater than the sum of the objectives, which requires synergy between the learning objectives. In this system, embedding opportunities to demonstrate the program learning outcomes within the courses facilitates the development of that synergy.

Table 17:    Program Learning Outcomes Data Summary – Program Level.

![image](images/C0391003T17.jpg)

*Target Population*: Students at the end of program.

*Unit of Assessment*: Program learning outcomes.

*Administration*: Faculty review of student learning outcomes demonstration.

*Frequency*: Every three years.

Table 18:    Program Learning Outcomes Assessment – Institutional Level.

![image](images/C0391003T18.jpg)

*Target Population*: Students at the end of program.

*Unit of Assessment*: Program learning outcomes.

*Administration*: Faculty review of student learning outcomes demonstration.

*Frequency*: Every three years.

[Table 19](#CH003.html#tab19) presents a tool to aggregate the learning objectives measurements into a formative assessment of the learning process in a program.

Each cell records the number of students who demonstrated each category of performance for each learning objective. The categorical counts summarize the data, and the categorical averages describe the program’s ability to deliver the learning objectives that construct each of its program outcomes.

Table 19:    Program Learning Objectives Data Summary Tool.

![image](images/C0391003T19.jpg)

![image](images/C0391003T19A.jpg)

![image](images/C0391003T19B.jpg)

![image](images/C0391003T19C.jpg)

![image](images/C0391003T19D.jpg)

![image](images/C0391003T19E.jpg)

*Target Population*: Students during coursework.

*Unit of Assessment*: Program learning objectives.

*Administration*: Faculty review of student learning objectives demonstration.

*Frequency*: Every three years.

It is also useful to explore the performance of the learning objectives collectively at the institutional level.

[Table 20](#CH003.html#tab20) presents a tool to aggregate the program learning objectives measurements by a program and summarize them to demonstrate the institution’s ability to deliver course learning objectives for all programs within the institution.

The Categorical Averages demonstrate the performance of the program learning objectives delivery at the institutional level. The Categorical Counts allow these data to be correlated with other functional performance data to search for relationships between institutional performance and learning objective delivery.

The search for these functional correlations necessitates the collection of descriptive data that do not presume a specific model so that the correlations may reveal unanticipated correlations between institutional performance and the attributes of the instructional system. If the data assume a specific theoretical perspective, the data become measurements of the theory and not the performance of the instructional system. Theories of learning engagement, experience, and achievement require testing to demonstrate their performance in a specific institution (Hurst, 2016). While these theories provide excellent conceptual frameworks for the design of the instructional system, their performance in each specific and unique learning environment requires measurement and demonstration. The methods and procedures in this systematic assessment approach make it possible to conduct such confirmatory research.

Table 20:    Institutional Learning Objectives Assessment.

![image](images/C0391003T20.jpg)

*Target Population*: Students during coursework.

*Unit of Assessment*: Program learning objectives.

*Administration*: Faculty review of student learning objectives demonstration.

*Frequency*: Every three years.

### **Measuring Program Feasibility and Viability**

As an open system organizational model, the contingencies of the external environment drive and constrain both programs and institutions. Institutional effectiveness is determined by the institution’s ability to address these contingencies in the design of its programs. It is possible for a program to be performing effectively internally but irrelevant or unnecessary from the perspective of the external environment. Without assessing the viability of the program to address the needs of the external environment, program performance improvement strategies can prove ineffective (Pritchard, Pausits, & Williams, 2016).

An institutional environmental scan identifies the contingencies at work in the external environment at the institutional level. These contingencies impact the design and performance of academic programs and requires measurement and assessment in the program design (Freeman, Chambers, & King, 2016).

The measurement and assessment of program viability compares two sets of data to describe the performance of each program: the contingencies of the external environment that are relevant to the program (from the environmental scan) and the response of the program design to those contingencies (from the mission, vision, and values of the institution) (Freeman, 2017). The relationship between these two perspectives systematically examines the feasibility of new programs or the viability of existing programs (Grosemans et al., 2017).

A GoF analysis is a research model that achieves such an analysis (Bozeman & Feeney, 2008; Demir, 2016; Shayan, Elahi, Ghazinoory, & Hoseini, 2018). The GoF analysis is a statistical method, which compares two sets of quantitative data to determine the extent to which those two sets are comparable.

When applied to program feasibility or viability, the analysis describes the degree to which the structure of an academic program aligns with the mission, vision, and values of an institution (Harrison, 2015). The GoF methodology provides a relevant conceptual framework to monitor the congruence between the program, the external environment, and the institution. These data are also compared with other functional data to uncover correlations between functional performance and the contingencies of the external environment.

The first component of a feasibility analysis is to determine the congruence between the program’s MVVs and the institutional MVVs. [Table 21](#CH003.html#tab21) presents a tool that measures the alignment or consistency of a program with the mission of the institution. For purposes of demonstration, this tool uses hypothetical institutional commitments of effectiveness, service to others, values-driven performance, human dignity, and inclusion as the constructs expected of all programs in the institution.

The evaluator examines the content of the curriculum (or proposed curriculum) to measure the alignment between a program and the constructs contained in the institution’s mission. The results are recorded as percentages, converted to decimals, and reported for each construct. In addition to monitoring the alignment of an existing program, these data are correlated with other functional performance data to uncover correlations between program attributes and institutional performance.

The second component of the program viability assessment is a measurement of the program’s alignment with the institution’s values. [Table 22](#CH003.html#tab22) presents an instrument to measure the degree of alignment between a program and institutional values. In this instance, the criteria are the institutional values of “educational innovation,” a “global perspective,” “ethical conduct,” “diversity and inclusion,” and “financial responsibility.”

Table 21:    Institutional Mission Alignment Measurement.

![image](images/C0391003T21.jpg)

![image](images/C0391003T21A.jpg)

![image](images/C0391003T21B.jpg)

*Assumption*: The design of this tool assumes that the alignment of a program with the mission of the institution in which it exists is a factor in program success. The correlations between these data and other performance data reveal the relationship between mission alignment and program performance.

*Target*: Individual program.

*Unit of Measurement*: Constructs in mission statement.

*Administration*: Administrator evaluation of program content.

*Frequency*: Every three years.

Table 22:    Institutional Values Alignment Measurement.

![image](images/C0391003T22.jpg)

![image](images/C0391003T22A.jpg)

![image](images/C0391003T22B.jpg)

*Assumption*: The design of this tool assumes that the alignment of a program with the values of the institution in which it exists is a factor in program success. The correlations between these data and other performance data reveal the relationship between values alignment and program performance.

*Target*: Individual program.

*Unit of Measurement*: Constructs in values statement.

*Administration*: Administrator evaluation of program content.

*Frequency*: Every three years.

Like the mission alignment measurements, the evaluator compares the content of the program curriculum with each construct to demonstrate the extent to which the program demonstrates the values of the institution.

Another component of viability measurement is an evaluation of the competitors for each program. This analysis includes the identification, definition, and market success for each similar program in the institution’s market.

[Table 23](#CH003.html#tab23) presents an instrument to use to identify and measure the program’s competition.

Column 1 identifies each competing program in the institution’s competitive focus area that offers a similar program. Column 2 records the name of the institution and Columns 3–7 report the credentials each program grants, the accreditations of the programs, current enrollments, tuition, and delivery modality in the appropriate column for each competing program. The market percentage is calculated by dividing the current enrollment for each program by the total enrollment of all programs in the institution’s area. These data provide a systematic measurement of similar programs, which studies the potential for success of an existing or new program.

A separate and discrete measure of the Target populations is also a critical component of a Viability measurement. [Table 24](#CH003.html#tab24) presents an instrument to measure and describe the target population for each delivery format of an existing or new program.

Table 23:    Competitor Measurement.

![image](images/C0391003T23.jpg)

*Target*: Program competitors.

*Unit of Measurement*: Competitor attributes.

*Administration*: Administrator evaluation of external program performance.

*Frequency*: Every three years.

The distribution of students by traditional aged versus non-traditional aged and online versus on ground delivery options measures the need for multiple delivery formats of a program. Many criteria may describe the population distribution. However, these factors study the relationship between the delivery format of the program and potential enrollments.

Table 24:    Target Populations Measurement.

![image](images/C0391003T24.jpg)

*Target*: Potential student populations.

*Unit of Measurement*: Number of potential students.

*Administration*: Administrator evaluation of market size.

*Frequency*: Every three years.

Table 25:    Market and Career Measurement.

![image](images/C0391003T25.jpg)

*Target*: Potential enrollments.

*Unit of Measurement*: Potential need for graduates.

*Administration*: Administrator evaluation of market size.

*Frequency*: Every three years.

From this information, the potential for a program to recruit and graduate students in the program market is calculated. [Table 25](#CH003.html#tab25) presents an instrument to measure the potential enrollments and program completions based on the size of the job market and past institutional performance. The focus of the market and the number of current programs offering an equivalent program are described (James, 2015a).

The average number of current jobs posted over a specific period is determined for the market area and multiplied by the program length to determine the number of potential positions that are available to graduates of the program. The focus market number is divided by the number of programs (including the existing or new program) to calculate the potential market share. The calculated market share is multiplied by the internal admissions conversation rate for similar programs in the institution to produce the predicted recruitment. The persistence rate is multiplied by the predicted recruitment to calculate the theoretical enrollments. The persistence rate is the index of the total values from values alignment measurement, which has consistently correlated with program persistence.

The final measurement of program viability is a measurement of the infrastructure needed to deliver the program. Infrastructure, in this usage, is the collection of institutional functions that must be engaged in the delivery of each program (Dougherty & Natow, 2015; Holbeche, 2017).

[Table 26](#CH003.html#tab26) presents a tool to measure the extent to which the resources currently available within the institution are adequate to deliver a program.

The Current Faculty calculations indicate the number of institutional faculty who have an interest and expertise in the discipline of the program. The Instructional Design Capacity, Student Services, Admissions, Financial Aid, and Career Services calculations seek to estimate the impact of the program on the current capacity of those functions.

This series of instruments measure the potential for success of a program based upon the internal and external conditions in which it exists. These measures provide authentic, trustworthy, and credible data to measure the feasibility and viability of a current or future program and its potential for viable performance using measurements of the external contingencies that impact each program. These data are also compared with other functional performance data to uncover correlations between program viability and functional performance.

### **Assessing Program Feasibility and Viability**

The assessment of program feasibility or viability is accomplished by summarizing and aggregating the measurement data into categories (Harper, 2011). [Table 27](#CH003.html#tab27) presents a tool to summarize data from the measurements of a program’s feasibility or viability. The tool summarizes the measurements collected with the instruments by category for each program.

The data collected in each of the measurement instruments are averaged and reported for each category of measurement as an index. This instrument demonstrates the viability of a program based on the measurements collected in the measurement process. These data are compared with other functional data to uncover correlations between external contingencies and institutional performance.

For purposes of decision making, it is essential to be able to compare the results of multiple programs to evaluate their potential for success. [Table 28](#CH003.html#tab28) presents a tool to aggregate the assessment data for multiple programs.

Each column reports a summary of one of the six data collection processes in the measurement phase. Each row reports the values for a single program, and the columns compare the values for each category across a series of programs. This approach to demonstrating program viability creates a systematic process for understanding the potential of a program to succeed. The data presented in this assessment are also correlated with other functional data to search for correlations between institutional performance and program viability (Casarejos, Gustavson, & Frota, 2017).

Table 26:    Infrastructure Measurement.

![image](images/C0391003T26.jpg)

*Target*: Institutional capacity.

*Unit of Measurement*: Institutional resources.

*Administration*: Administrator evaluation of available resources.

*Frequency*: Every three years.

Table 27:    Program Feasibility Data Summary.

![image](images/C0391003T27.jpg)

Table 28:    Program Feasibility Assessment.

![image](images/C0391003T28.jpg)

*Target*: New or existing programs.

*Unit of Measurement*: Program attributes.

*Administration*: Administrator evaluation of program attributes.

*Frequency*: Every three years.

## Chapter 4

## **Measuring and Assessing Instruction: Intended Performance**

### **The Instructional System**

An instructional system includes all aspects of the learning environment with which a student interacts to obtain the learning articulated by the program. This system includes the content and structure of the curriculum, the design of the course components, instructional strategies, learning resources, as well as instructional support (Clouder, Cureton, & Steventon, 2016; Rubenstein & Ridgley, 2017). The assessment of the instructional system requires the measurement of the students’ interactions with all components of the instructional system to determine their ability to deliver the intended learning (Lecca, 2015). The best evidence of this effectiveness is student perceptions of the extent to which the components of the instructional system helped them learn (Allen & Nichols, 2017; Bell & Aldge, 2014; Macfadyen & Dawson, 2012).

The instructional system includes four distinct categories of interactions: learning engagement, learning experience, learning achievement, and learning support. These categories interact synergistically to produce a dynamic learning system and measuring each of these constructs makes it possible to study the interactions between these categories. This section examines the direct measurement of the instructional system. The next section details the measurement and assessment of the Instructional Support Services. A systematic assessment measures each of these functions separately and aggregates those measurements into an assessment of the entire instructional system. This approach generates authentic, credible, and trustworthy data that are correlated with functional and operational data to uncover relationships between institutional performance and program attributes.

For purposes of this demonstration, it is necessary to develop a conceptual framework to guide the definition of the instruments and resulting measurements. Instruction is a process, which is framed at a high level in the Institutional Vision Statement and more specifically in an Instructional Vision statement, which may exist at the institutional level, or, more appropriately for each discipline.

### **Instructional Vision**

An instructional vision articulates the categories of methods and strategies that the faculty employs to facilitate learning. These strategies (processes) create the learning engagement, learning experience, and the learning environment (support). The instructional vision provides the faculty with a palette of differentiated instructional strategies that are adapted to serve the learning needs of diverse cognitive styles.

The Instructional Vision statement focuses on those specific characteristics of the instructional system that translate the instructional vision into instructional performance. The hypothetical Instructional Vision Statement upon which the instruments presented in this section are based is:

Instruction in this institution differentiates the design, delivery, and measurement of learning to accommodate the cognitive styles of the learners. The instructional staff identifies and employs strategies to engage learners, facilitate an effective learning experience, and create an environment that is supportive of the learner’s efforts to learn.

This statement provides a broad conceptual framework that is adaptable to each discipline to design and develop instruments to measure the effectiveness of the components of the instructional system.

### **Measuring Learning Engagement (Classroom Observations)**

Learning engagement is defined as the interactions between faculty, course components, and other students in the process of acquiring or accessing information. These interactions determine the ability of the students to acquire the information they need to achieve the expected learning. One of the principle ways students engage with the learning process is through their interactions with the instructor, both inside and outside the classroom (Scheerens & Blömeke, 2016; Taut & Rakoczy, 2016). The structure and format of the course components, textbooks, media, etc., impact the ability of the students to engage with learning (Heikonen, Pietarinen, Pyhältö, Toom, & Soini, 2017).

To measure these interactions requires several measurements, beginning with a method to objectively measure the interactions between faculty and students during an instructional experience. The purpose of this measurement process is to collect trustworthy and credible measurements that describe the type and frequency of the interactions during an instructor-led instructional event (Kuh et al., 2000).

There are two aspects of this information that it is vital to collect: the qualities or modes of the interactions and the distribution of those modes over the learning experience. These data are correlated with institutional data to uncover correlations between performance and the attributes of classroom interactions.

The interactions between faculty and students fall into at least six discrete and generic categories of engagement: the instructor is presenting information, the instructor is asking a question, the instructor is answering a question from the students, the students are presenting information, the students are asking a question, the students are answering a question. These categories provide the constructs to differentiate and record the strategies, distribution, and rate of changes over time in instructor interactions.

[Table 29](#CH004.html#tab29) presents an instrument to measure the learning engagement strategies in a learning event through observations of the interactions between the instructor and students (Purcell, Scott, & Mixson-Brookshire, 2017; Scheerens & Blömeke, 2016). For purposes of this demonstration, the six conditions serve as the criteria for the observations.

This measurement tool records data that describe the activities and behaviors of an instructor in a real-time classroom. The instrument asks the observer to identify the kind of interactions that are taking place at one-minute intervals. Using an interval timer or a watch with a second hand, the observer records the interaction occurring at one-minute intervals. This approach produces a systematic sample of the interactions, which objectively describe the interactions.

This instrument is expanded to include a more significant number of observations or other criteria of interaction such as teaching effectiveness models (Scheerens & Blömeke, 2016). The administration of the primary tool does not require any specialized training or knowledge of learning theory; it observes generic and discrete interactional behaviors. If the tool is expanded to include teaching models (Fisk, 2016), the observers need training in the theory and practice of those models.

Table 29:    Learning Engagement Observation Form.

![image](images/C0491004T29.jpg)

*Data Source*: Instructors’ classroom interactions

*Unit of Measurement*: Interactions.

*Administered*: Observations of Instructors during class interactions.

The purpose of this instrument is to describe the qualities of the interactions between the instructor and the students, rather than to judge the efficacy of the instruction. These data directly (authentically) measure the profile of those interactions. It is the qualities of the learning engagement that are being measured and not the instructor or the instruction. The resulting data describe the performance of classroom engagement strategies and reveal correlations between institutional performance and the effectiveness of the engagement strategies when compared with other functional data.

### **Measuring the Learning Experience (Course Components Measurement)**

The interaction between the student and the instructional components of the course in addition to the interactions with the instructor measured above define the learning experience (Chianese, 2015). The interactions between the student and the components of the instructional system generate the learning experience. The purpose of these measurements is to provide a trustworthy and credible description of the effectiveness of the student interactions with the instructional components. The interactions with some of these components contribute to learning engagement and others contribute to the characteristics of the learning experience and the resulting learning environment.

The measurement of the learning experience requires measurements of the ability of all components of the instructional system to impact the learning needs of the students. The impact of these components on learning is information that is only known to the student and collected from each student (Blair & Valdez Noel, 2014; Grebennikov & Shah, 2013; Seale, 2010). This questionnaire measures the qualities of the interactions of the students with the course components and not the instructor.

[Table 30](#CH004.html#tab30) presents a questionnaire to students in or at the end of a course to collect the student’s perceptions of the ability of each component to facilitate the learning objectives and outcomes. In this situation, a questionnaire is a useful instrument to use to gather these measurements.

A questionnaire is an instrument that asks the respondent to evaluate a situation that is external to themselves through direct questions, as opposed to a survey which tends to collect subjective opinions (Bowman-Bowen & Menard, 2016). The questions in a questionnaire are more highly structured and focuses the attention of the respondent on their response to a specific stimulus.

The information collected via this Learning Experience Questionnaire organizes the information into the constructs of course design, the instructional system, and the evaluation of learning. The questions (content) measure those constructs based on instructional systems design assumptions. Each question asks the student to describe how well the course components helped them learn the course material.

This instrument generates data that describe the effectiveness of the course components to help each student achieve the learning objectives. The instrument makes no assumptions about the meaning or appropriateness of the responses; it is designed only to describe the ability of the course components to support the learning of the course objectives for everyone. With these data, the faculty and staff evaluate that information to assess the effectiveness of the learning experience. These responses are also correlated with institutional performance data to uncover relationships between institutional performance and course attributes.

Table 30:    Learning Experience Questionnaire.

![image](images/C0491004T30.jpg)

![image](images/C0491004T30A.jpg)

*Assumption*: The design of this tool assumes that the student perception of the ability of the learning experience to deliver the program learning objectives is a factor in student success. The correlations between these data and other performance data reveal the relationship between the attributes of the learning experience and program performance.

*Target Population*: Students’ experiences in each course.

*Unit of Measurement*: Instructional Interactions.

*Administered*: Students in specific courses.

### **Measuring Learning Achievement (Portfolio, Prior Learning Measurement)**

The final factor measured to study the effectiveness of the instructional system is a measure of the learning achievement generated by that instructional system. The learning that results from the student’s interactions with the instructional system measure the ability of the students to demonstrate the intended outcomes in their work products. To measure learning achievement requires data that measure both the program outcomes and program (course) learning objectives (Penn, 2011). The measurement of these outcomes is a separate process from a course grading and conducted with a random sample of work products, to minimize faculty workload through generalizability.

The faculty evaluates the sample of work products using a well-constructed rubric to determine the extent to which students demonstrate the learning outcomes of the program and the learning objectives in the courses. These measurements focus on the results of the instructional system and not the performance of individuals.

The measurement of learning outcomes is conducted effectively with selected student work products compiled into a developmental portfolio. The learning artifacts are either compiled during the program coursework or generation by a unique project course, such as a capstone or topics course. As part of the measurement process, students choose the artifacts they think demonstrate each outcome.

In the model presented here, the program outcomes and the program learning objectives define the constructs and content measures for each program learning outcome, which is one section of the portfolio. A measurement rubric assists the faculty with gathering trustworthy and credible measures of the program learning outcomes. [Table 31](#CH004.html#tab31) presents a model of a Student Learning Outcomes Portfolio, which measures the outcomes of a hypothetical program in General Psychology.

Column 1 identifies the program outcome to be measured. Column 2 identifies the criteria (from program learning objectives) that define and measure the program outcome from Column 1. Column 3 asks the student to identify the artifact they have chosen for evaluation, and Column 4 asks the student to explain how that artifact demonstrates the outcome. Each student demonstrates each criterion in ways that are specific to them and their interests. This type of instrument does not seek to find right vs. wrong answers but to provide each student with an opportunity to demonstrate each criterion in ways that are meaningful (authentic) to them.

Table 31:    Student Learning Outcomes Portfolio.

![image](images/C0491004T31.jpg)

![image](images/C0491004T31A.jpg)

![image](images/C0491004T31B.jpg)

*Assumption*: The design of this tool assumes the integration of program learning objectives into program outcomes and measured by a student portfolio to articulate the interconnections between program learning objectives and outcomes for both the student and the evaluator. The correlations between these data and other performance data reveal the relationship between student achievement and instructional delivery.

*Data Source*: Student learning outcomes demonstration.

*Unit of Analysis*: Instructional system.

*Administered*: Student populated with artifacts from coursework or prior learning.

The data collected from this instrument monitor the function of learning achievement as demonstrated by the learners in a program. Student achievement is a critical component of institutional effectiveness (Kuh et al., 2015; Oaks, 2015).

### **Prior Learning Measurement**

An added benefit of this process is that this portfolio can also conduct a Prior Learning Measurement using the same criteria as those that evaluate the work of students who pursue the program. In the case of a Prior Learning Measurement, the student submits artifacts from their work life or past academic activities, etc., to demonstrate each criterion and outcome. The criteria are used to evaluate the artifact are the same for both assessments, which ensures the Prior Learning Measurement is equivalent to the evaluation of work products from students who complete the academic program.

In summary, this series of measurements provide trustworthy and credible data to monitor the function of the instructional system to engage students in learning, facilitate learning, and deliver the program learning outcomes. These data are also correlated with other functional data to reveal the relationships between student achievement and the attributes of the instructional system.

### **Assessing Learning Engagement (Observations)**

The learning engagement data contain information that informs two dimensions of student engagement: the variation of interaction strategies and the rate or pattern of change over time. In this example, the collected data presume no preferred model of engagement; they measure the interactions observed during student–instructor interactions. These data summarize and aggregate into an assessment of one session of interactions, which demonstrates the interaction strategies used by the instructor and the distribution of those strategies across time. This model may also be compared with a preferred model of interaction that the faculty presumes to be optimal (Houser & Frymier, 2009).

[Table 32](#CH004.html#tab32) presents the measurement instrument populated with hypothetical data, to describe one specific class session.

This tool visualizes the data for an individual class and reveals both the distribution of interactions as well as the rate of change. The summary in the last column counts the number of instances of each strategy observed, and this information is used to calculate the percentage of use for each strategy. This visualization reveals the profile of learning engagement the instructor facilitated in a class session.

The data for a series of classes are summarized and aggregated for each instructor to describe the collective performance of an individual instructor (Walker & Freeze, 2011). This aggregation reveals the approach to learning engagement in use by each instructor.

[Table 33](#CH004.html#tab33) presents a tool that describes the learning engagement strategies used by an instructor.

Table 32:    Learning Engagement Data Report.

![image](images/C0491004T32.jpg)

*Assumption*: The design of this tool assumes that the distribution and rate of change of interactions between an instructor and a group of learners drive and constrain student engagement. The correlations between these data and other performance data reveal the relationship between instructional delivery and student engagement.

*Target Population*: Single classroom.

*Unit of Assessment*: Classroom interactions.

*Administration*: Compilation and aggregation of data.

Table 33:    Distribution of Engagement Strategies for an Individual Instructor for Multiple Courses.

![image](images/C0491004T33.jpg)

*Target Population*: Individual instructor.

*Level of Analysis*: Learning engagement model.

The categorical averages are calculated from the categorical counts and aggregated into an assessment of each instructor. These data may also be aggregated at the program level to reveal the learning engagement strategies experienced by the students during each program. In this case, the categorical counts summarize the percentages of use for each category of strategy within one program.

[Table 34](#CH004.html#tab34) presents an aggregation of the data tool to describe the learning engagement strategies by the program.

In this assessment, the total counts for each instructor are summed to provide the number of counts for the total program by category of interaction. The counts calculate the percentages of each category of interaction experienced by the students within a program.

These data compare the Instructional Vision statement of the institution with the compliance of the learning engagement efforts with the institutional Vision statement.

Lastly, the measurement data are aggregated into an assessment tool to describe the learning engagement strategies throughout the institution. This assessment compares the learning engagement strategies across the institution with the institutional Vision statement to describe institutional compliance with the institutional Vision.

[Table 35](#CH004.html#tab35) presents the measurement data aggregated into an assessment of the instructional vision of the institution.

This battery of assessment enables the demonstration of the faculty learning engagement strategies from multiple perspectives in the institution, creating a summary of the faculty learning engagement strategies. In addition to the faculty observations, the data are compared with other functional data to uncover correlations between the attributes of learning engagement and institutional performance. This systematic approach to the assessment of the learning engagement strategies reveals the contribution of the learning engagement strategies for the effectiveness of the institution.

### **Assessing the Learning Experience (Course Components Assessment)**

The data collected in the Course Components Questionnaire, which measures the ability of the course components to facilitate the learning of students in each course, assesses the learning experience. These data describe the effectiveness of the design of the course to deliver the learning objectives to the students in each course. [Table 36](#CH004.html#tab36) presents a tool to summarize and aggregate the data collected from students in individual courses.

The data from all students are aggregated to demonstrate the effectiveness of each course component to facilitate the learning of the population of students currently pursuing the course. This effectiveness varies as student populations differ from class to class. However, this difference across time and populations ensures that the assessment includes the perspectives of a diversity of cognitive styles, which creates comprehensive conclusions.

Table 34:    Learning Engagement Data Summary.

![image](images/C0491004T34.jpg)

*Target Population*: Organizational groups of instructor.

*Level of Analysis*: Program.

Table 35:    Learning Engagement Data Aggregation.

![image](images/C0491004T35.jpg)

*Population*: Institution.

*Level of Assessment*: Institution.

*Frequency*: Yearly.

Table 36:    Learning Experience Data Report.

![image](images/C0491004T36.jpg)

![image](images/C0491004T36A.jpg)

![image](images/C0491004T36B.jpg)

*Target Population*: Students × Course.

*Unit of Assessment*: Individual course.

*Administration*: During a course session.

*Frequency*: End of each course.

The categorical counts are summarized for each dimension of the learning environment: learning engagement, learning experience, and learning achievement. The categorical averages in this model describe the effectiveness of the course. The categorical counts provide additional aggregations and comparisons with operational data to uncover correlations between institutional performance and the attributes of the instructional system.

These data are also aggregated into an assessment of the effectiveness of the courses within a program. [Table 37](#CH004.html#tab37) presents an assessment tool that summarizes and aggregates the measurement data into an assessment of all courses within a program.

Table 37:    Learning Experience Data Summary.

![image](images/C0491004T37.jpg)

*Target Population*: Courses x Program.

*Unit of Assessment*: Engagement, experience, and achievement.

*Administration*: Calculated for each academic session.

*Frequency*: End of each session.

The categorical counts for each course create averages for each course in the three categories of learning engagement, learning experience, and learning achievement. The categorical averages for all courses evaluate the courses within a program, or other groupings of courses and the categorical averages for all courses are compared with institutional performance data to uncover correlations of program course attributes with other aspects of institutional performance. This approach aggregates the raw data from individual courses taught by multiple instructors into a summary of all courses within a program, which demonstrates the effectiveness of the instructional system within a specific program.

[Table 38](#CH004.html#tab38) presents an assessment tool that aggregates the measurement data into measures of all courses within the institution. These data describe the overall effectiveness of the instructional system at the institutional level and are compared with other functional data to determine the correlations between the attributes of the instructional system and institutional performance.

The categorical averages evaluate the effectiveness of all programs within the institution. The total counts for the institution are compared with other institutional performance data to uncover correlations between institutional performance and specific attributes of the total instructional system.

Table 38:    Learning Experience Data Aggregation.

![image](images/C0491004T38.jpg)

*Target Population*: Institution.

*Unit of Assessment*: Engagement, experience, and achievement.

*Administration*: Calculated for each academic session.

*Frequency*: End of each session.

The search for these correlations necessitates the collection of data without presuming a specific model so that the correlations may reveal unanticipated relationships between institutional performance and the attributes of the instructional system. Most theories of engagement, experience, and achievement are theories that have not been tested under the conditions at work in a specific institution. While these theories provide a conceptual framework to design the instructional system, their effectiveness to each specific and unique learning environment require measurement and demonstration. The instruments created in this system provide the data to conduct those evaluations.

These assessments demonstrate the effectiveness of the total instructional system through the aggregation of credible and trustworthy data authentically collected from the best evidence available, the students who experience the system. The dataset produced by this series of measurements can be mined to address specific research questions about the performance of the instructional system.
