## Chapter 5

## **Measuring and Assessing Support Services: Intended Performance**

### **Defining Student Support Services**

Like all components of the organization of an institution, the student support services require a conceptual framework in the form of mission, vision, and value statements to facilitate performance and measure results (Perl & Noldon, 2000). This organizational or strategic plan may be more critical for support services since services are processes and not products. Additionally, support services assist students with their specific needs, and the solutions and results are not standardized. The ability to systematically monitor, demonstrate, and enhance the performance of these processes is critical to institutional success (Pickering & Sharpe, 2000).

Student support services aid students in three areas of responsibility: experiential learning experiences, instructional and learning support, and transition support. Within these categories is an impressive collection of specialized areas to support the diversity of people and ideas and the contribution of the student support services to institutional success (Chambers, 2011; Holder, 2007; Levy & Polnariev, 2016).

There are many models of student services organization that design these functions (Ellis, 1998; 2010). For purposes of these demonstrations, the following statements provide the conceptual framework for the measurement of student services effectiveness.

[Table 39](#CH005.html#tab39) presents the template to design and articulate the mission, vision, and values of a student support services function within an institution of higher education.

In this example, the institutional mission, vision, and values translate into the student services functions under the dimensions of experiential learning, instructional support, and transition services. The procedure for creating this model is the same as that for creating the institutional mission, vision, and values, but constrained by the institutional statements. The constructs identified in these statements frame the measurements of performance collected for each of the components of the student services function with content that is specific to each area of responsibility ((Harrison, 2015) Maloshonok & Terentev, 2016).

Table 39:    Mission, Vision, and Values for a Student Services Function.

 
|   || **Outcomes** || **Statements** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Mission:  
Instructional and learning support experiences || Promote student success  
Support the development of students  
Engage students in learning experiences  
Facilitate an experiential learning experiential || The mission of the student support services is to promote student success, support the intellectual development of students, engage students in learning, and facilitate experiential learning experiences for all students |
| Vision:  
Experiential learning experiences || Foster communication and community  
Continuing education (lifelong learning experiences)  
Uphold the integrity of the learning experience  
Keep students informed about processes to succeed  
Coaching  
Tutoring  
Constructive problem solving || We deliver our mission through learning events, fostering campus-wide communication, continuing education, upholding the integrity of their learning experience, and informing students about processes to help them succeed. We accomplish these tasks through effective coaching, tutoring, and constructive problem solving |
| Values  
Transition support || Successful students  
Believe in learning as a lifetime event  
Successful graduates  
Community activist  
Secure families  
Performance  
Respect student time constraints  
Understand the student perception of their plight || We engage in these activities because we value successful students, lifelong learning, successful graduates, community activists, and secure families. To accomplish our mission and vision, we focus our efforts on performance, respecting the time limits of our students, and understanding the student’s perception of their plight in life |

Because the target of these measurements is support services, it is necessary to understand the impact of these services on student learning. The students are the authentic source and best evidence of the ability of each function to serve their specific needs. Therefore, the instrument is measuring the effectiveness of the process (serving their needs) and not the ultimate result, which is impacted by many factors other than student support services (Ellis, 1998).

These instruments ask the students to report their perception of how each function has helped them learn and succeed. The student’s perceptions of the effectiveness of the services to equip and motivate them to succeed are a critical component of the “support” function (Chambers, 2011; Holder, 2007; Levy & Polnariev, 2016).

### **Measuring Satisfaction**

In these instruments, student perceptions provide the measures of satisfaction through questions that model one or more of the empirical constructs of satisfaction. While “are you satisfied with” is an acceptable question, for institutional effectiveness, it produces inadequate information to improve institutional performance.

Questionnaires are instruments that collect personal and individualized information in a structured or framed manner (Sheu, Mejia, Rigali-Oiler, Prime, & Chong, 2016). A questionnaire asks convergent questions that focus on the effect of an external stimulus to produce a desired internal response, unlike surveys that tend to ask divergent questions about an internal result (how happy are you with …). In this measurement process, the objective is to measure the effectiveness of the strategies by the services staff, whatever those were, to achieve the results the student expects and needs to succeed, without specifying them (Dumitrescu & Martinsson, 2016).

The assessment of job satisfaction has confirmed the measurement of satisfaction (Dawis, 2004). The job satisfaction models provide a systematic, science-based approach to the measurement of satisfaction that is adapted to use for student support services (Bowling, Wagner, & Beehr, 2018; Forero & Gómez, 2017; Sheu et al., 2016).

There are five empirical models of satisfaction that are adapted to scale and measure satisfaction in student support services functions (Dawis, 2004). These are:

1.  The fulfillment of needs – did the strategies and methods provide the guidance needed to succeed, for example, instructional support services.
    
2.  The fulfillment of values – did the experiences reflect and model the stated values, for example, social experiences.
    
3.  The fulfillment of expectations – did the experiences deliver the expectations created for it, for example, transition support services.
    
4.  The perception of equity – did the experiences assist all students, cultures, and genders with equal respect and assistance, for example, diversity and intercultural services.
    
5.  Social information processing – did the experiences create an opportunity for group or experiential learning, for example, community learning experiences.
    

Questions framed by these models of satisfaction measure the effectiveness of the student support services. The questions in each instrument include examples from all five models of satisfaction to match the intended contribution of the service to the success of the students. Each of the instruments uses the same “Likert-type” scale to help the respondents measure their responses (Uebersax, 2006).

### **Measuring Experiential Learning Services**

Many strategies deliver experiential learning; most of these intend to create a community of learning experience (Levy & Polnariev, 2016). These experiences provide a practical context for the learning in each discipline as well as enhancing the instructional experience to include activities that are global (social information processing) in scope. Relevant activities provide immediacy to the learning experience for all programs as well as generate a culture of learning for the institution (Kennedy-Phillips, Baldasare, & Christakis, 2015; Lee, 1995). The role of experiential learning experiences is to enhance the learning experiences of the total population (Bauer, Stevenson, & IGI, 2014; Fink & Hummel, 2015).

Special presentations, campus life events, or intern/externships, delivers the experiential learning component of the student services function to engage students in the academic community (Pomerantz, 2006; Terkla & O’Leary, 2014).

For this demonstration, the primary concept common to all student support services is “the support of learning,” and in this case, community or collective learning (Kooy, 2015; Wilson, Gochyyev, & Scalise, 2017). [Table 40](#CH005.html#tab40) presents a questionnaire to measure the contribution of a student life office, which manages the experiential learning component of the student support function in this hypothetical institution.

This questionnaire asks respondents to report their perceptions of the adequacy of the student life services to meet their expectations and create a meaningful (to them) community learning experience. Not all students have the same needs, so the answer to the questions respect the personal needs and interests the individual students bring to the situation and does not provide a measure of goodness or badness but a description of the services provided to meet their interests and needs. This approach accepts the students as they are and where they are in their development and seeks to move them forward toward their individual goals.

The resulting measurements represent the individual perceptions of the offerings’ ability to advance each student’s learning. Therefore, the measurement is of the services’ ability to “fulfill the needs” of all students in the institution, which is a direct measurement of the ability of the service to “support” the needs of all students. These data monitor the effectiveness of the student life services and are compared with other functional data to uncover correlations between institutional performance and the attributes of student life performance.

The internship/externship experience is a strategy to expand the student experience to interact with the professional community and include the professional experience as a component of the academic experience (Moore, 2014). [Table 41](#CH005.html#tab41) presents a questionnaire to measure the contribution of the internship/externship experience to the experiential learning component of the student support function.

Table 40:    Student Life Measurement.

![image](images/C0591005T40.jpg)

Assumption: The design of this tool assumes that student life activities are a component of the creating of community learning experiences. The correlations between these data and other performance data reveal the relationship between student life offerings and student support services delivery.

Target population: Students who have attended the student life office events.

Unit of measurement: Student satisfaction with events.

Administration: Service users.

Frequency: Every session.

Table 41:    Measurement of Internship/Externship Experiences.

![image](images/C0591005T41.jpg)

![image](images/C0591005T41A.jpg)

Assumption: The design of this tool assumes that the qualities of the internship/externship experience are a factor in the delivery of the experiential learning component of the students support services function. The correlations between these data and other performance data reveal the relationship between the internship experience and student support services function.

Target population: Students who have participated in an internship/externship.

Unit of measurement: Student satisfaction with internship/externship.

Administration: Service users.

Frequency: Every session.

As a component of experiential learning, the qualities of the internship/externship experience to provide the opportunity to explore the profession is a direct measurement of that component’s contribution to the experiential learning experience component of the student support services function. This instrument measures the ability of internship/externship programs to provide a learning experience that “fulfilled the expectations” of the individuals who participated.

The resulting data monitor the ability of the internship/externship service to provide access to meaningful external learning experiences. These data are compared with other functional performance data to reveal correlations between institutional performance and the attributes of the internship experience.

### **Measuring Student Learning Support Services**

Learning support services exist in many forms and delivery methods to accommodate the cognitive styles of diverse student populations. A discrete process measures the ability of the learning support services to promote and enhance each students’ ability to learn by providing personalized strategies and solutions (York, Gibson, & Rankin, 2015). These instruments measure the ability of the instructional support services to facilitate and impact student learning in those individuals who use the services. [Table 42](#CH005.html#tab42) presents an instrument to measure the contribution of the academic success center to the instructional support component of the student support services function.

Table 42:    Academic Success Center Measurement.

![image](images/C0591005T42.jpg)

Assumption: The design of this tool assumes that the qualities of the academic success center are a factor in the delivery of the learning support services component of the students support services function. The correlations between these data and other performance data reveal the relationship between the success center performance and the student support services function.

Target population: Students who have used the academic success center.

Unit of measurement: Student satisfaction with academic success center.

Administration: Service users.

Frequency: Every session.

This questionnaire asks students who have used the services to identify how effectively the services provided by the academic success center assisted them with their individual learning challenges. For these services to be used and useful, they must be accessible to the student’s physically as well as timely. The resulting data monitor the effectiveness of the academic success center service to support student learning needs.

The library delivers a similar yet separate function within the student support services. The *American Libraries Association* has developed and published standards for libraries in institutions of higher education. These standards provide the content in the instrument presented in [Table 43](#CH005.html#tab43), which measures the contribution of the library to the instructional support component of the student support services function.

The Library questionnaire asks students to evaluate the accessibility and availability of the library facilities and services to assist them with their individual learning. These library services may be available physically or virtually and the questions are the same for both conditions as the service provided contributes effectively to both delivery methods.

Table 43:    Library Questionnaire.

![image](images/C0591005T43.jpg)

![image](images/C0591005T43A.jpg)

Assumption: The design of this tool assumes that the qualities of the library are a factor in the delivery of the learning support services component of the students support services function. The correlations between these data and other performance data reveal the relationship between the library performance and the student support services function.

Target population: Students who have used the library.

Unit of measurement: Student satisfaction with library.

Administration: Service users.

Frequency: Every session.

These data monitor the effectiveness of the Library service to fulfill the learning needs of students. The data are compared with other functional data to reveal correlations between institutional performance and the attributes of the library function.

An essential and required service for students is the availability of accommodations for special needs students. These services separately inform a variety of reports to various regulatory agencies and conducted with only the students who use these services. The questions here are related to the availability of physical access as well as learning access.

[Table 44](#CH005.html#tab44) presents an instrument to measure the contribution of disability services to the instructional support component of the support services function.

The services identified in the disabilities services measurements are those required by federal agencies and the *Americans with Disabilities Act*. In many states, there are additional requirements articulated by the state in which the institution resides, and this instrument must include these specialized requirements. The resulting data monitor the effectiveness of the disabilities service to ensure that students with disabilities have access to the learning experiences available to all students.

Table 44:    Disabilities Services Measurement.

![image](images/C0591005T44.jpg)

Assumption: The design of this tool assumes that the qualities of the disabilities services are a factor in the delivery of the learning support services component of the students support services function. The correlations between these data and other performance data reveal the relationship between the disabilities services performance and the student support services function.

Target population: Students who have used the disabilities services.

Unit of measurement: Student satisfaction with disabilities services.

Administration: Service users.

Frequency: Every session.

A final service that is also a regulatory requirement is the presence and availability of counseling services by professionally trained and licensed counselors. The focus of these services is academic counseling, which helps students succeed academically and to navigate the learning environment. [Table 45](#CH005.html#tab45) presents an instrument to measure the contribution of the counseling services to the instructional support component of the student support services function.

Table 45:    Counseling Services Measurement.

![image](images/C0591005T45.jpg)

Assumption: The design of this tool assumes that the qualities of the counseling services are a factor in the delivery of the learning support services component of the students support services function. The correlations between these data and other performance data reveal the relationship between the counseling services performance and the student support services function.

Target population: Students who have used the counseling services.

Unit of measurement: Student satisfaction with counseling services

Administration: Service users.

Frequency: Every session.

The counseling services office provides support and guidance to students with a variety of performance needs. This questionnaire asks students who have used the services to evaluate the effectiveness of the services to help them succeed socially as well as academically. The resulting data monitor the effectiveness of the counseling services to support the access and learning needs of the students who seek help.

This battery of instruments measures the effectiveness of a sample of the instructional support components of an institution to serve the needs and interests of the students who avail themselves of those services. Each component is separately measured, and the resulting data monitor the performance of that service. These data are also compared with functional performance data to uncover correlations between institutional performance and the attributes of the student support services function.

### **Measuring Student Transition Support Services**

The final component of the student support services function is to support the transition of students from academic life to their next life experience, which may be personal, professional, or educational. The outcome of the student’s experience may be successful employment, further education, or other life experiences and the student services function must support all eventualities. Measuring the ability of the transition support services component to assist students with their transition from the institution to their next life experience is a critical part of the effectiveness of the student support services function.

An Office of Career Services or Placement in many institutions offers transition services. This office prepares students to obtain employment, further education, or a wide array of other experiences as they leave the institution. The data from this office are a vital component of accreditation and regulatory requirements for most institutions, which often provides services for many years after the students have graduated. The questionnaire presented in [Table 46](#CH005.html#tab46) asks the students to report the contribution of a hypothetical career services office to the transition component of the student support services function.

The instruments discussed thus far collect data focus on the specific contributions of each service and component of the student support services function. In addition to these specific data, it is useful to have data collected from students who have completed their program of study and experienced student support services from a more global and complete perspective. Comparison of these two sets of data often reveals surprising differences that identify prolific enhancements to the services, components, and the function.

The graduate student support services questionnaire is administered to graduating students and asks them to reflect on their total learning experience and report how well the support services contributed to their success. Questionnaires conducted on a periodic basis during their experience often show negative results since the services are incomplete when measured. At the end of the academic experience, the students are in a much better position to evaluate the contribution of the services to their total success. [Table 47](#CH005.html#tab47) presents a questionnaire to measure the overall contribution of the student support services function to their success.

The graduate survey asks students to provide a global assessment of the student support services to support their learning experiences. These data monitor the success of the overall function and are compared with other functional performance data to reveal correlations between institutional performance and the attributes of the student support services function.

This battery of instruments provides data that comprehensively measure the contributions of the student support services function to student success. While these data intend to assess the contribution of each component of the function, the data are also compared with other functional data to determine the correlation between functional performance and the specific attributes of the student support services function. These correlations often uncover unanticipated relationships and provide dynamic data to inform improvement decisions.

Table 46:    Career Services Measurement.

![image](images/C0591005T46.jpg)

![image](images/C0591005T46A.jpg)

Assumption: The design of this tool assumes that the qualities of the career services are a factor in the delivery of the transition services component of the students support services function. The correlations between these data and other performance data reveal the relationship between the career services performance and the student support services function.

Target population: Students who have used the career services.

Unit of measurement: Student satisfaction with career services.

Administration: Service users.

Frequency: Every session.

While empirical frameworks and theories develop strategies to help students, trustworthy and credible data collected from authentic sources demonstrate their effectiveness in a specific environment (Duque, 2016). This demonstration is especially relevant to student support services which are managing strategies differentiated by the complexities of learners and their cognitive styles.

### **Assessing Student Support Services**

The assessment of the student support services is accomplished by summarizing and aggregating the measurement data into categories defined by the constructs contained in the support services mission, vision, and values. These data models create multiple views of the data, which demonstrate the intended performance of the support services at the service, component, functional, or institutional levels (Henning & Roberts, 2016).

Table 47:    Graduate Student Support Services Measurement.

![image](images/C0591005T47.jpg)

Assumption: The design of this tool assumes that the qualities of the student support services function are a factor in the successful program completion of students. The correlations between these data and other performance data reveal the relationship between the student support services performance and student program completion.

Target population: All graduating students.

Unit of measurement: Student satisfaction with student support services.

Administration: Service users.

Frequency: Every session.

The service level assessment summarizes the measurement data for each of the individual services and describes the performance of each service with its constituents. These data demonstrate the effectiveness of the service to the individuals who use it. The component level assessments aggregate the measurement data into the categories of the three components of the support services function: experiential learning, instructional support, and transition services. The functional level assessments aggregate the measurement data for each program to demonstrate the performance of the student services function to the different programs it serves. The purpose of these multiple views is to demonstrate the intended performance of the student services to the students, the components, the function, and effectiveness of the institution. These data are also compared with other functional data to uncover correlations between institutional performance and the attributes of the function.

### **Experiential Learning Assessments**

The experiential learning component of the support services function delivers the student life and the internship/externship experiences services. These two services are assessed discretely and together to demonstrate their individual performance and their contribution to the larger purpose of creating an experiential learning experience component of the student support services function.

[Table 48](#CH005.html#tab48) presents a summary of the responses of student users and describes the contribution of the student life office to create the experiential learning component of the student support services function for each student.

The categorical counts row presents the total counts for each performance category, and these data calculate the categorical averages for each performance category. Other assessments aggregate the categorical counts into categorical averages, which demonstrate the performance of the service.

These data are re-aggregated to demonstrate the contribution of the student life office for each academic program. The specific needs of each group of students vary by the nature and structure of the academic discipline in which the students are engaged. Therefore, the same strategies that work for a liberal arts program may not meet the needs of students in advanced science programs.

[Table 49](#CH005.html#tab49) presents an aggregation of the raw measurement data for each program to explore the contribution of the student life service to the success of students in different programs.

The same approach is used for all the components of student support services to assess the component, the student services function, and the institution.

The process of aggregating the data for each service by “different” programs generates the ability to study the correlations between the performance of the same strategies to different cognitive styles of students. The differences or disagreements provide an opportunity to advance the understanding of the performance and effectiveness of each student service (Binswanger & Oechslin, 2015; Snehal & Kremer, 2010; van Woerkom & Sanders, 2010; Young, Alibali, & Kalish, 2012).

[Table 50](#CH005.html#tab50) presents a tool to summarize the data collected from individual users of the internship/externship component of the experiential learning experience of the student support services function.

This summarization of the individual responses demonstrates the effectiveness of each service to meet the needs of the students it serves.

Unit of assessment: Student life office.

Administration: Calculated from raw data.

Frequency: Yearly.

Table 49:    Student Life Component Assessment.

![image](images/C0591005T49.jpg)

Target population: Student users of component × program.

Unit of assessment: Student life office.

Administration: Calculated from raw data.

![image](images/C0591005T50A.jpg)

Frequency: Yearly.

[Table 51](#CH005.html#tab51) presents a tool to aggregate the data from the internship/externship users by the program to demonstrate the ability of the internship/externship experience to fulfill the needs of the individual users in each academic program.

The aggregated categorical counts in these tables demonstrate the performance of the student experiential learning component contribution to the support services function in the final assessments. Collectively, the student life component and the internship/externship services deliver the experiential learning component of the student support services function.

### **Student Transition Support Services**

A centralized career services office delivers the student transition support function. This office is solely responsible for these services in this hypothetical model of student support services under consideration.

[Table 60](#CH005.html#tab60) presents a tool to summarize the individual user responses and assess the contributions of the career services office of the transition services component of the student support services function.

[Table 61](#CH005.html#tab61) presents a tool to aggregate student individual user data into a demonstration of the performance of the career services office to provide the transition services component of the student support services function for each program.

The student transition services measurement data are compared with functional data to uncover correlations between institutional performance and the attributes of the transition services.

[Table 62](#CH005.html#tab62) provides an assessment to demonstrate the contribution of the student support services to the three components of the student support services function.

### **Global Student Services Assessment**

The global assessment of the contribution of the student services functions to students in the institution summarizes the Graduate Students Services Questionnaire data. This measurement is conducted at the end of the program to provide a summative assessment of the contribution of these services to the student experience.

The global data aggregated in this table are compared with the data in the individual component assessments to uncover correlations or inferences that may emerge from the differences in the responses to these multiple perspectives. These assessments are framed by the articulated intentions of the services to provide the service and used by the student support services staff to demonstrate the effectiveness of the student support services function.

These data are also compared with functional data to uncover correlations between institutional performance and the attributes of the student support service function.

In the student support services assessment, the individual data monitor the performance of each service, the aggregated data demonstrate the effectiveness of the component and service to the programs, and the global data compare the responses of graduating students (summative assessment) with the students “in progress,” a formative assessment. Together, these assessments provide a comprehensive demonstration of the effectiveness of the student support services.

## Chapter 6

## **Functional Data Modeling: Identifying the Drivers and Constraints of Actual Performance**

### **Overview**

In this phase of systematic assessment, the functional data are analyzed to identify the KPIs for each function. These KPIs are grouped into causal triads with their two strongest cross-functional correlates to identify the drivers and constraints of functional performance (Goldratt, 1990). The strongest of these KPIs demonstrate the performance of the functions of the institution. This procedure is the first of two phases of the data modeling process in which the purpose is to create multi-level models of institutional performance (Antony & Lott, 2012) from a comprehensive set of credible and trustworthy functional data (Ledder, 2016).

### **Functional Performance Data**

In the previous chapters, instruments collect trustworthy, credible, and authentic data to measure the performance of functional systems at the level of the transaction, or point of service, between the institutional functions and the stakeholders. These data describe the results of the individual processes and procedures the function uses to accomplish its work and provide the best evidence of the performance of the functional systems to serve the needs of its constituents.

Over 90 performance indicators (measurements) are measured. Individually, these measurements describe the performance of the function with individuals and not the collective performance of the function. The individual functional data are aggregated for each indicator to demonstrate the intended performance of each function.

### **Functional Data Modeling**

Data modeling is a multidimensional process of searching for and uncovering the mathematical relationships that exist within large data sets (Antony, & Lott, 2012; He, Levine, Fan, Beemer, & Stronach, 2018). In this application, the data models reveal the emergent interrelationships in the functional performance data and translate that information into meaningful models of actual performance unframed by the MVVs (intended performance). The MVVs define what the function does, that is, intended performance. The data modeling uncovers what the system is doing, that is, actual performance. The interrelationships revealed in the modeling process between the indicators (correlations) represent the “synergies” of cross-functional performance that construct institutional performance.

In the measurement phase, performance data were summarized to monitor the performance of each function with the stakeholders. In the assessment phase, these individual data were aggregated to demonstrate the performance of the function at multiple levels of the institution. The data modeling processes transform the functional data into models of institutional performance that demonstrate the effectiveness of the institution.

In this first phase of data modeling, these interrelationships are calculated by using correlation coefficients to quantify their size and relationship (Cook & Zhu, 2014; Cook et al., 2014). The correlations are calculated between all indicators in the measurement data (Peral et al., 2017). The second phase of the data modeling groups these interrelationships into authentic models of institutional performance by applying the organizational effectiveness approaches as a conceptual framework for each model (Cameron & Whetten, 2013).

An exploratory factor analysis is conducted with the functional data set (all responses to all indicators) to analyze the functional data for its interrelationships by calculating the correlations between all indicators. While it is possible to use many data modeling methods and statistics in this phase, the exploratory and confirmatory factor analyses are particularly useful approaches because they identify the plausible relationships within the functional data and combine those correlations into descriptive models. The factor analysis reports a matrix of correlations between all indicators (variables) as well as using the variance in the data to calculate models of factors, which explain performance with the fewest indicators.

The total functional data set contains the individual responses to each of the 90 performance indicators measured in the functional systems. Each variable (curricular structure, course efficiency, etc.) is conceived only as a performance “indicator” (not KPIs) at this phase of the process. Since these are categorical data, the factor analysis use a *Spearman Rho* coefficient of correlation (Kalkan & Kelecioglu, 2016; Plewis & Raab, 1999), which measures the size of the correlations (synergies) between each indicator and every other indicator as well as the direction of the correlation. The result of this analysis is a 90 × 90 correlation matrix, which contains a total of 8,100 correlations. This matrix is too large to evaluate performance in a meaningful way, and it is necessary to reduce (transform) these 8,100 calculations into models of the data that most completely explain the performance of the institution. The factor modeling function reduces these correlations to the minimum set of indicators that fully define institutional performance.

The correlation matrix identifies the performance indicators with the strongest correlations, either positive or negative, within the functional data. The strongest correlations become the KPIs (Kucukaltan, Irani, & Aktas, 2016). Positive KPI correlations indicate a driver of performance and negative KPI correlations indicate a constraint on performance (Goldratt, 1990, 2009; Goldratt & Weiss, 2005; Techt, 2015).

### **Aggregating Functional Data into Causal Triads**

As has been discussed, single indicators are inadequate to demonstrate the complexities of institutional performance. In this example, there are 90 performance indicators, suggesting that any single indicator only explains 1/90th of the total performance information. The indicators are triangulated into meaningful groups to provide practical meaning. In this assessment system, the KPIs are clustered into triads to demonstrate performance. The correlations between the KPIs define these triads based on the strength of the correlations. These triads demonstrate the interrelationships between the indicators, which represent the synergies of functional performance (Nagarajan & Maluk Mohamed, 2017).

A subset of the data is analyzed to calculate the KPIs between indicators and demonstrate the procedure with all 90 indicators. [Table 65](#CH006.html#tab65) presents a correlation matrix reported from a factor analysis conducted with the subset of 14 indicators selected from the 90 indicators contained in the original functional data set.

[Table 65](#CH006.html#tab65) presents the correlations between each of the 14 indicators and each of the other 14s indicator. With this information, it is possible to identify the strongest indicators that are varying together or “covarying.” A correlation below –0.400 and above +0.400 is considered a strong correlation, while those between –0.400 and +0.400 are considered weak correlations. A correlation of or around zero indicates no correlation. The strongest correlations become the KPIs of institutional performance because they represent the most powerful drivers of institutional performance.

Each KPI with its two strongest correlates (positive or negative) triangulates into clusters of three KPIs or KPI triads. Each triad contains the primary indicator and its two highest correlating indicators either positive or negative. The clustering procedure is a method of calculating and revealing the synergies of functional performance (Drouin, Stewart, & Van Gorder, 2015; Tewari, Singh, & Tewari, 2016).

[Table 66](#CH006.html#tab66) presents the results of the clustering of the KPIs calculated in the correlation matrix reported above. Those correlations presented in bold are positive correlations, and those in italics are negative correlations. The correlation for each indicator and a description of the relationship is included to translate the meaning of the cluster into functional performance.

[Table 66](#CH006.html#tab66) identifies the two strongest correlations for each indicator. Some of the resulting triads contain strong correlations; others do not. When used to describe institutional performance, the data model excludes triads with weak correlations from the data model. In the systematic assessment of performance, this analysis creates information that program staff and managers use to determine the causes of actual functional performance.

Table 65:    Correlation Matrix (Spearman *Rho*).

![image](images/C0691006T65.jpg)

Table 66:    KPI Clusters Based on the Strength of Correlation.

![image](images/C0691006T66.jpg)

![image](images/C0691006T66A.jpg)

### **First-, Second-, and Third-Order Effects of Functional Changes**

The clustering process also provides a method to anticipate the consequences of changes made to a system to enhance or correct performance. In the field of organizational learning, these are first-, second-, and third-order effects of a change in strategy (Aminbeidokhti, Jamshidi, & Hoseini, 2016; Börjesson Rivera, Håkansson, Svenfelt, & Finnveden, 2014; Noumair & Shani, 2018; Pinelli & Maiolini, 2017; Robinson, 2001).

The first-order effects are those that occur at the level of the change. In [Table 66](#CH006.html#tab66), columns 2 and 3 identify the plausible first-order effects anticipated for changes in the indicators in column 1. That is, if the curricular structure–external validity produces a positive change, a first-order effect would be the changes expected on the indicators that correlated most strongly with external validity: *curricular structure–coherence* and *disabilities services*. Since both correlations are negative, a positive change in the *curricular structure–external validity* indicator plausibly produces a negative change in the curricular structure–coherence and disabilities services indicators.

Second-order effects of a change in the curricular structure–external validity would vary with the KPIs associated with disabilities services, the strongest KPI associated with changes in the curricular structure–external validity. The disabilities services indicator negatively correlates with curricular structure–external validity, which is a first-order effect, and with the course effectiveness indicator. Therefore, a second-order effect of a positive change in the curricular structure–external validity is anticipated for the course effectiveness indicator, in the negative direction, since the correlation with the primary indicator, curricular structure–external validity, is negative and the correlation between disabilities and course effectiveness is positive. The size of the correlations predicts the likelihood of such a consequence.

There are also effects on the indicators associated with each of the indicators included in the second-order effects; these are third-order effects. The third-order structure–effects of a change in the curricular structure–external validity would be those expected in the indicators associated with the second strongest correlation. In this example, the third-order effects would be curricular structure–coherence, which positively correlates with program learning objective and tutoring performance. Since the correlation between curricular structure–external validity and curricular structure–coherence is negative, a positive change in curricular structure external validity indicator produces a negative impact on the curricular structure–coherence indicator as well as the program learning objective and tutoring performance indicators since they correlate positively with curricular structure–coherence.

This method provides the staff and managers with information to anticipate the consequences of changes to the functional systems they are considering, which informs the action-planning process. Whether there is a real effect is determined after the change is implemented and measured with the same instruments that identified it (Harmon et al., 2018; Noumair & Shani, 2018). In the systematic assessment of complex, nonlinear, and dynamic processes, these calculations intend to provide plausible contingencies to consider when identifying the first, second, and third order consequences of a change in strategy.

### **Summarizing Functional Data Over Time**

Often it is desirable to study the performance of a system over time to better understand the results of changes made to the functional system (Harmon et al., 2018). The performance averages calculated in the assessment process compares the actual performance data for multiple years. This strategy reveals trends in the data for years, as well as the long-term effects of changes to the functional system. When implementing a change strategy, the immediate response to the change is usually negative. If the change strategy produces a positive change, that change emerges over the long term.

[Table 67](#CH006.html#tab67) presents a dashboard of the functional performance of all programs using the calculated KPIs over five years. This dashboard presents a view of the actual performance of a function of the institution over five years.

The KPIs fall into three functional summaries: the learning environment, the instructional system, and student outcomes (learning and life). These three categories mirror the institutional commitments articulated in the mission, vision, and values of the institution. In this example, 19 strong functional KPIs were selected and sorted into the three categories to provide a high-level description of the actual functional performance of the institution. This process transforms intended functional performance data into actual functional performance data.

A scorecard is most easily comprehended when it employs a maximum of three categories of analysis and no more than seven KPIs for each category (Fooladvand, Yarmohammadian, & Shahtalebi, 2015). This design standard is supported by the principles of absolute judgment in the field of neuroscience, which studies the characteristics and abilities of the human information processing (sensemaking) system (Alan, 1994; Linhares et al., 2011; Miller, 1956; Shiffrin & Nosofsky, 1994) to make sense of these indicators. The traditional conclusion of this research indicates that three categories (channels) of seven indicators each is the maximum number of indicators humans process effectively, which is an assumption factored into the design of the measurement and assessment instruments.

These data models focus on describing and demonstrating performance at the functional level. In the second phase of data modeling, the functional KPIs are reorganized into the organizational effectiveness approaches (Antony & Lott, 2012; Ashraf & Kadir, 2012) to create authentic models of the performance of each function to the institutional level.

### **Institutional Data Modeling**

In this second phase of data modeling, the KPIs that emerged from the first phase of functional data modeling are reorganized into organizational effectiveness models to develop an institutional view of functional performance. The data modeling approach reorganizes the information into the institutional effectiveness approaches to demonstrate institutional performance from multiple perspectives (Brown, 2017; Harrison & Shirom, 1998; McClure, 2017; Tadesse, Manathynga, & Gillies, 2018). These models group the KPIs of actual functional performance into models of institutional performance, differentiated by the characteristics of the processes each function uses to perform its mission (Dey, Hassanien, Bhatt, Ashour, & Satapathy, 2018). This second facilitate of data modeling provides visual data models to facilitate the sensemaking process for institutional effectiveness (Morgan, 1996).

Table 67:    Institutional Dashboard Summarizing Functional Assessments.

![image](images/C0691006T67.jpg)

The effectiveness models facilitate the process of “looking beyond the functional performance data” to see the actual performance of the institution and uncover implicit strategies to consider for performance enhancement (La Salle & Johnson, 2016; Vinyard et al., 2017; Macfadyen & Dawson, 2012).

To fully understand the performance of the institution in its environment, dimensions of operational performance (efficiency) are also useful to include in the institutional performance models. These data are collected by the systems that monitor the daily transactions outside the functions of the institution.

The sensemaking process may combines functional and operational data into models of institutional performance using the same data modeling methods used the functional data. The resulting KPIs reveal the drivers and constraints of the actual performance at the institutional level (Goldratt, 1990).

Five organizational effectiveness approaches have been researched, tested, and confirmed as appropriate models to demonstrate the contribution of functional educational systems and processes to institutional performance. These approaches are the goal-path approach (often identified as the goal-attainment approach), the systems approach, the strategic-constituencies approach, the strategic contingencies approach (Hickson, Hinings, Lee, Schneck, & Pennings, 1971), and the competing-values approach (Rukh & Qadeer, 2018). Each of these approaches assumes a distinctly different functional process and reorganizes the functional KPIs into data models, which demonstrate institutional effectiveness for institutional sensemaking.

*Goal-path approach*. The goal-path approach assumes effectiveness as the achievement of a clearly defined goal, or goals, which produces identical outcomes that demonstrate specific criteria. This approach is most credible to functions or institutions that are deliberate, rational, goal-seeking efforts using predictable processes to produce identical results from consistent inputs. The goals may fall into short- or long-term categories and must be specific, measurable, and shared.

Designed for manufacturing applications where the result is identical products, such as the manufacturing of electronic instruments, it is a useful approach for those aspects of institutional performance that require uniformity. In this systematic assessment of educational institutions, this approach is applied to the effectiveness of the design of program curricula and courses.

*Systems approach*. The systems approach assumes effectiveness as generated by establishing and maintaining processes and procedures that produce a consistent result. Effectiveness in this approach is demonstrated by producing outcomes that demonstrate specific, yet complex criteria in ways that are unique (within limits) to each product; in the case of education, individualized products. This approach explains the performance of an organization by demonstrating the integrity of three functional processes: inputs, throughputs, and outputs.

The system processes are interactive, interdependent, and causal (synergistic); therefore, the KPIs require a degree of interconnectedness produced in this system by calculating the KPIs directly from the correlations between the indicators. Commonly employed in process-oriented functions, this approach is consistent with the characteristics of a functional component that obtains resources with varied characteristics from the external environment, treats or processes those resources in a structured manner, and returns those resources to the external environment with new or improved characteristics. It is the process that is measured and assessed.

In this systematic assessment, the systems approach is used to make sense of the performance of each academic program to institutional performance.

*Strategic constituencies approach*. The strategic constituencies approach assumes that stakeholders’ satisfaction with the efforts of the institution to serve their individual needs is evidence of effectiveness (Alfirević, Pavičić, & Mihanović, 2007; Börjesson et al., 2014; Holbeche, 2017; Nellesen, Birnbaum, & Greenberg, 2010). The strategic constituencies approach measures the satisfaction of the stakeholder groups that are large enough to generate sufficient statistical power to be a discrete group. Stakeholder groups that are not large enough are combined with other groups until the power is sufficient and the analysis includes all stakeholder groups, no matter how small.

This approach ensures that the efforts (design assumptions) of the institution are serving the needs (real performance) of all stakeholders by evaluating the results with diverse constituencies. In this systematic assessment process, the strategic constituencies’ model informs the design of the measurements and assessments of the student support services function.

*Strategic contingencies approach*. The strategic contingencies approach to organizational effectiveness assumes that the effectiveness of the institution is produced by effectively delivering the outcomes or results from each function in the institution (Junqueira et al., 2016; McAdam, Miller, & McSorley, 2016; Qiu, Luo, Jackson, & Sanders, 2017). It makes sense of the performance of an institution on its strategic plan. In this systematic process, the strategic contingencies approach was used to design the measurements and assessments of the instructional system to deliver the program learning outcomes and program learning objectives. In this application, the strategic contingencies are the program outcomes the institution intends to deliver. The program learning objectives provide the tactics and strategies that deliver those outcomes. The strategic contingency approach is consistent with the processes employed by the instructional system to deliver the learning for the institution (Doyle & Seagren, 1984).

While this approach is like the goal-path approach, it is strategically different. The goal-path approach evaluates a product (curriculum); the strategic contingencies approach evaluates a process (learning).

*Competing-values approach*. The competing-values approach combines many of the attributes of the previous approaches formulated in a much more flexible and less precise manner. This approach assumes that institutional effectiveness is determined by how well the institution manages competing interests and forces with broad and flexible outcomes (Grabowski, Neher, Crim, & Mathiassen, 2015; Rukh & Qadeer, 2018). Effectiveness measures the response to a condition without seeking to change the condition.

The competing-values approach makes sense of institutional performance by modeling the KPIs into a stimulus–response format. In this systematic model of assessment, the competing-values approach used to design the environmental scan, which measures the expectations of the external environment.

These approaches apply to organizational effectiveness in functions that demonstrate the same assumptions as each effectiveness approach. Any approach may be applied to any aspect of institutional performance to match the institution’s conception of itself and the way it wishes to assess its performance. The use of these approaches as data models facilitates the process of “looking beyond” the functional data systematically to make sense of institutional effectiveness and provides a collection of methods to analyze and organize functional data to make sense of institutional effectiveness.

### **Conceptual Underpinnings**

These approaches differentiate by the assumptions and thinking systems that underpin them (Cresswell, 2006). Since these models engage the collective intelligence of the institution into the review, evaluation, and sensemaking process, it is useful to articulate the differences in the ways these approaches conceive of performance.

The goal-path model and the strategic contingencies approach are both dualistic thinking models. They assume that for every problem, there is one correct solution. Both conceive of effectiveness as a dichotomous event; it is an either/or assessment. This approach is most useful to an institution or function that views its performance dualistically or mechanically.

The systems approach assumes that for every problem there are a few viable solutions. This approach represents multiplistic thinking and presents a more flexible approach to institutional effectiveness. This approach works well for those institutions that view their performance as process- or procedure-driven with slight variations needed to accommodate unique situations with slight variations.

The strategic constituencies approach reflects a relativistic thought process. In this approach, for every problem, many solutions work, depending on the circumstances. This approach applies a conceptual framework to a highly varied and unpredictable operational system, to limit its performance options. It is most appropriate to organic institutions in which functional performance is adapted to the individual needs of the stakeholders (Duque, 2016).

The competing-values model accommodates flexibility and variation on both the stimulus (problem) side and the solution side of the process. The competing-values approach assumes that institutional performance is complex and composed of a series of changing circumstances to which the institution responds in complex ways. The complexity exists on both sides of the equation, and this approach is appropriate to organic systems, which exist in complex, unpredictable, and dynamic external and internal environments. In this conception of effectiveness, the performance of the institutional response is measured and assessed for effectiveness using the functional data.

These differentiated models make sense of institutional effectiveness as the institution conceives itself to be. Each model applies to multiple situations depending upon the conception the institutional leaders choose to assume.

## Chapter 7

## **Institutional Data Modeling: Looking Beyond the Data**

### **Overview**

In this phase of the process, the KPIs calculated in the initial data modeling phase are reorganized into the models of organizational effectiveness to make sense of the institutional performance of each function and its contribution to the performance of the institution. In this procedure, the functional performance KPIs, which are framed by the MVVs, are transformed into institutional performance KPIs and differentiated by the institutional effectiveness frameworks. The use of the organizational effectiveness approaches to model the KPIs creates an authentic model of institutional performance from multiple perspectives (Fortuin, van Koppen, & Leemans, 2011).

### **Making Sense of Functional KPIs Through the Goal-Path Approach**

The *goal-path* approach to organizational effectiveness (also known as the goal-attainment approach) configures the KPIs to facilitate sensemaking for functions in which the contribution is to produce clearly defined outcomes with identical attributes. The result of a goal-path approach is to produce an identical product with limited variance from the criteria. The degree to which the product complies with the standards established by the institution determines the effectiveness.

The *goal-path* approach has two components: a specific goal or outcome that accomplishes and the processes and protocols which produce that outcome. The evaluation criteria define a specific model, standard, and criterion to measure the completed product. The goal-path approach presumes linear, stable, predictable, and uniform contributions of the function to institutional effectiveness. The assumption that underlies a goal-path approach is that performance is a dualistic event: the goal was achieved/not achieved; the agreed upon strategy, criteria, or method was followed/not followed (Casarejos, Gustavson, & Frota, 2017; Moran, 2016).

### **Using the Goal-Path Approach to Assess Effectiveness**

In the systematic assessment of the institution under discussion here, the goal-path approach makes sense of the structure of program curricula and courses to institutional effectiveness. The goal is to design curricula that demonstrate specific design attributes, which produce effective curricula. The path is the attributes (criteria) the curricula demonstrate, which creates that effectiveness.

Both the curricula and the courses follow processes and procedures researched and developed for the practical design and delivery of learning in higher education. The designers, faculty, and staff determine the specific way those attributes manifest in the curriculum design. For this application, the KPIs demonstrate the compliance of the design of the curriculum with those design standards and criteria.

In the goal-path approach, the results evaluate the compliance with the design criteria and not the performance of the curriculum or courses. [Table 68](#CH007.html#tab68) demonstrates the application of the goal-path approach to making sense of the design attributes of one program curriculum.

This criterion reports the percentage of a curriculum that demonstrates each of the criteria to assess the structure of the curriculum and the courses in one program. In each cell is the index or average for each design criterion. The program learning outcomes KPIs are reported for comparison and correlation with the structural KPIs. This comparison provides an indicator of the effectiveness of each criterion.

[Table 69](#CH007.html#tab69) demonstrates a tool for compiling these KPIs into larger groups (schools, divisions, disciplines, etc.) to make sense of the performance of the curricula over time as well as its trends. This information demonstrates the effectiveness of the design of the curricula to serve the needs of a changing student population.

The table presents the performance of the curriculum for the past four academic years (AYx). The goals column establishes an initial goal of 90%, which is the expected level of compliance for each component.

The goal-path approach reveals the extent to which the curricula in all programs demonstrate the established standards (Blouch, Ulrich, & Michenzi, 2016).

This modeling reveals the degree of compliance as well as areas that are not in compliance. From these areas of non-compliance emerge the opportunities to enhance the design of the curriculum and the program (Ladyshewsky and Taplin, 2015). Overall, this technique provides an authentic assessment of the contribution of the curricula to institutional performance and through an appropriate use of the goal-path approach to effectiveness.

### **Making Sense of Assessment KPIs through the Systems Approach**

The systems approach to institutional effectiveness facilitates sensemaking for an institution or function, which focuses on the processes that deliver the institutional mission, vision, and values to stakeholders. The systems approach ignores external factors in performance and attends to those factors that are the responsibility of the institution to directly evaluates the vision of the institution. It assumes effectiveness is demonstrated by the processes and procedures it follows in performance (Erichsen et al., 2013; Hart & Paucar-Caceres, 2017; Tadesse et al., 2018). In this example, the systems approach makes sense of the performance of the instructional programs to contribute to institutional performance.

Table 68:    Goal-Path Approach to Sensemaking.

![image](images/C0791007T68.jpg)

![image](images/C0791007T68A.jpg)

Table 69:    A Trending Analysis Using the Goal-Path Approach at Institutional Level.

![image](images/C0791007T69.jpg)

In a systems approach, the functional KPIs are clustered into three dimensions of performance: inputs, throughputs, and outputs. These three dimensions are the acquisition of stakeholders, the education or training of stakeholders, and the success of those stakeholders after leaving the institution.

The effectiveness of the processes used to acquire stakeholders (inputs) requires more than a quantitative measurement of the number of stakeholders admitted to the institution (Sullivan & Wilds, 2001). It also includes the performance of those students as well as the ability of the institution to serve the needs of those stakeholders. The evaluation of the measurements of the input processes demonstrates the ability of the institution to attract the kind of students it requires, to attract stakeholders whose needs the delivery systems can serve, to attract stakeholders who persist, succeed, and benefit from the intentions of the institution. In summary, the effectiveness of the input process is demonstrated by the performance of the stakeholders (people) who enter and experience the institutional processes.

The throughputs of a systems approach to sensemaking measure the performance of the processes and procedures the institution uses to deliver its mission. This dimension of the systems approach evaluates the effectiveness of the design of the curriculum, the course components, and the engagement, experience, and outcomes produced by those processes. The throughput process evaluates the design of the program to deliver what it says it delivers to the students who populate the program.

The output of the systems approach to sensemaking studies the effectiveness of the input and throughput processes to deliver the outcomes it articulates. The ability and motivation (Deci, 1975) of the stakeholders to persist through the total program (successfully or unsuccessfully), to enter society with the knowledge, abilities, and values the institution says it delivers, and to persist in their chosen life-path are KPIs that demonstrate the effectiveness of the performance of the institution.

The systems approach to sensemaking demonstrates the effectiveness of the instructional vision at the program level. Through extension, it also assesses the effectiveness of the institutional vision.

To make sense of the program performance data to institution performance, 21 key performance indicators distributed across the three categories evaluate program effectiveness, based on the 3 × 7 rule (Alan, 1994; Linhares et al., 2011; Miller, 1956; Shiffrin & Nosofsky, 1994). To adapt this approach to the institutional level, 21 institutional level KPIs distributed into the same three categories of performance. Some of the indicators may be relevant to both evaluations; others are relevant to only one condition. For the assessment of the institutional vision, the indicators are global and focus on the collective performance of all programs to contribute to the performance of the entire institution.

[Table 70](#CH007.html#tab70) uses the constructs organized as inputs, throughputs, or outputs as the basis for making sense of each dimension of institutional effectiveness. For each construct, one key performance indicator is chosen, its source identified, and it is defined.

Table 70:    Identification and Articulation of the KPIs for the Institution.

![image](images/C0791007T70.jpg)

![image](images/C0791007T70A.jpg)

![image](images/C0791007T70B.jpg)

The KPIs also track performance over time to determine the long-term difference between performance and goals, the size of the difference in percentages, and the trend over time. [Table 71](#CH007.html#tab71) presents such a report using the KPIs selected for this sensemaking process.

This strategy sorts the key performance indicators into the institutional categories of student acquisition (inputs) student experience (throughputs), and student success (outputs). The columns AY1–AY4 refer to the academic year and reports the past four years for this exercise. The *Δ* Goal (difference with the goal) column identifies the difference between current performance (AY4) and the goals established for each indicator. The *Δ* % column identifies the percentage of the difference between current performance and the goal, and the final column identifies the trend over the past four academic years.

With this trending report, institutional leaders understand the performance of the institution over time as demonstrated by the KPIs calculated for each year in the four years. This is an authentic application for the systems approach to effectiveness in an educational institution.

### **Making Sense of Assessment KPIs Through the Strategic Contingencies Approach**

The strategic contingencies approach measures the results of the functional system’s efforts to produce an articulated outcome. Compared with the goal-path approach, the strategic contingency approach has more flexibility and a broader and less rigid definition of success. For instance, in the goal-path model, the expected outcome is precisely defined, and all outcomes are intended to be identical. In a strategic contingencies approach, the outcome is broadly defined, and all outcomes are equivalent. The strategic contingencies approach applies to organic processes, such as student learning and success to demonstrate their effectiveness (Coates, 2014; Kuh et al., 2015).

In this demonstration, the strategic contingencies model of sensemaking applies to the functions involved in the delivery of the program learning outcomes. [Table 72](#CH007.html#tab72) demonstrates the application of the strategic contingences method for making sense of the program learning objectives KPIs to institutional performance.

In this tool, the averages of all objectives for all programs are recorded in the AY1–AY4 columns to demonstrate the percentage of students in the institution that are demonstrating the program learning objectives during their coursework. The average performance over the previous four years and the trend over that period are also presented. The trend is calculated by averaging the performance of the past four years and comparing that average to AY4 value. The difference is the current trend and direction. This application of the strategic contingencies model demonstrates the effectiveness of the courses in each program and the institution to deliver the “parts” of the program outcomes.

The same method makes sense of the student demonstrations of the program outcomes. The averages of all outcomes for all programs are recorded in the AY1–AY4 columns to demonstrate the percentage of students in the institution that are demonstrating the program learning outcomes for their program of study. [Table 73](#CH007.html#tab73) presents a format for this calculation following the strategic contingencies model of sensemaking.

In this method, the program learning outcome KPIs are modeled through the strategic contingencies approach to demonstrate the performance of the students in each program to demonstrate the program outcomes. This demonstration presents an authentic use of the strategic contingencies model of institutional effectiveness in an educational institution.

### **Making Sense of Assessment KPIs through the Strategic Constituency Approach**

In the systematic assessment method under study, the Student Support Services function demonstrates the functional attributes assumed by the strategic constituencies approach to institutional effectiveness (Connolly, Conlon, & Deutsch, 1980).

The strategic constituencies approach compiles the KPIs calculated from the measurement of stakeholder satisfaction into a data model to make sense of the effectiveness of the components of the student support services as well as the entire function. Since the objective is to determine how well the support strategies work for each constituency, the KPIs are calculated separately for each constituency. The comparison of the KPIs by constituency demonstrates the relative effectiveness of the student support services for all constituencies (Alfirević et al., 2007; Duque, 2016; Smith & Parker, 2005).

The constituency grouping is a calculated decision. Constituency groups that do not demonstrate sufficient power individually are clustered with other constituent groups to generate sufficient power. As student services focus on the processes that support student performance and achievement, rather than outcomes, the KPIs demonstrating performance at the institutional level are transactional.

[Table 74](#CH007.html#tab74) presents a tool for analyzing the student support services KPIs, collected through the instruments created earlier and sorted into the three areas of institutional responsibility identified for the support services.

In this tool, the KPIs for each of the three areas of institutional responsibility of the Student Support Services function is summarized and presented for four strategic constituencies. Differences in the performance between constituencies provide compelling information to help understand and improve the performance of the individual components of the student support services function. The averages of all constituencies for each of the three responsibility areas monitor the performance of the function across academic years.

[Table 75](#CH007.html#tab75) presents a tool that describes the overall contribution of the student support services to institutional performance level using the constituent averages. The table presents the average KPIs for all programs by academic year and a trend analysis calculated to demonstrate the performance of those KPIs over time.

Trend analyses are conducted in many ways, but the recommended approach for this system is to average all four years and compare the AY4 score with that average. The difference shows the size and direction of the trend for each of the institutional responsibilities. The Strategic Constituencies approach is an authentic method of making sense of the contribution of the Student Support Services to the MVVs of the institution (James, 2015b).

### **Making Sense of Assessment KPIs Through the Competing-Values Approach**

The competing-values approach to organizational effectiveness makes sense of environmental scan results. In this competing-values approach, the KPIs are selected to demonstrate the effectiveness of the internal response to the external contingencies (Chidambaranathan & Regha, 2016; Pounder, 2000; Rizvi & Lingard, 2011). Most often, this approach evaluates the interactions between large complex institutions and the external environment in which they exist (Kroeger & Weber, 2015; Qiu et al., 2017). The number of contingencies within each category of environmental concern is limited to a maximum of seven contingencies (Alan, 1994; Linhares et al., 2011; Miller, 1956; Shiffrin & Nosofsky, 1994).

Half of the KPIs collected for a competing-values assessment is measured internally (program feasibility and viability assessments) and the other half is collected externally (institutional environmental scan) (Volberda et al., 2012). The external KPIs objectively describe the conditions in the external environment, which impact institutional performance. The internal KPIs describe the response of the institution to those external contingencies.

The sensemaking process organizes the KPIs to demonstrate the effectiveness of the institutional response to address the external contingencies and expectations. In the competing-values approach, the presence of a reasonable and effective strategy to balance the competing interests of the external environment with the commitments of the institution determines effectiveness. In this competing-values approach, there is no presumption of right/wrong, appropriate/inappropriate, or any other linear paradigm. Instead, the presumption is that the external environment creates conditions that the institution attempts to address, which may be successful or unsuccessful.

In practice, it may be that the institution is unable or unwilling to address these concerns due to institutional commitments, that is, the mission, vision, and values. This condition creates a cognitive dissonance between the institution and the external environment, which is recognized and articulated, but unavoidable. However, this cognitive difference constructively uncovers strategies that advance the interests of both sides (Woerkom & Sanders, 2010; Snehal, & Kremer, 2010). Conducted in this manner, the competing-values model can significantly advance the position of an institution in a conflicted environment (Qiu et al., 2017).
