In the systematic process under consideration, the competing-values approach organizes the KPIs collected from the program feasibility and viability assessments, which compares external expectations with the attributes of internal programs. [Table 76](#CH007.html#tab76) presents an example of this KPIs summary and aggregation report generated from the program viability assessments.

In this application, the KPIs are summaries of the evaluations of existing programs, which makes this a viability analysis. The same method applies to the consideration of new programs in which case it would be a feasibility analysis. The values in each category are calculated from the individual measurements in the feasibility assessments and aggregated into the institutional assessment (Volberda et al., 2012). These provide the internal KPIs needed for the competing-values assessment.

Table 74:    Strategic Constituency Analysis of Student Support Services KPIs.

![image](images/C0791007T74.jpg)

Table 75:    Institutional Student Support Service Responsibilities.

![image](images/C0791007T75.jpg)

Table 76:    Internal Feasibility KPIs Summary and Aggregation for All Programs.

![image](images/C0791007T76.jpg)

[Table 77](#CH007.html#tab77) presents the KPIs that measure the external environment on the factors contained in the environmental scan. In [Table 77](#CH007.html#tab77), the external contingencies column contains the concerns of the external environment identifying three concerns per factor in this example. The internal response column contains the response of the internal systems to each of those contingencies. The assessment column identifies the KPIs that demonstrate the effectiveness of the institutional response. The assessment column identifies the KPIs for each internal response.

It is possible to quantify these calculations, and this is often a strategy used in a competing-values analysis in the form of a multi-dimensional or phenomenological scale. However, with that effort comes an implied “goodness or badness” to the numbers, which conflicts with the assumptions of a competing-values model that both the condition and the response are respected as good faith behavior. The KPIs (calculated in the clustering process) reported in the assessment column demonstrate the effectiveness of the internal efforts to address the external contingencies.

The scan and assessment of the external environment may be the most overlooked assessment in institutional research, due to the lack of a methodology for systematic sensemaking. The competing-values approach is an authentic method to make sense of the institution’s situation with the external environment to explain, enhance, or improve institutional effectiveness as well as similar applications to educational institutions (Grabowski et al., 2015; Gulosino, Jones, & Franceschini, 2016).

Collectively, these five reports present differentiated data models of the performance of each function and their contribution to the synergy of institutional effectiveness. The visualization of this synergy emerges from the KPI clustering strategy, which represents the performance interdependencies (synergy) from the factor analysis. These synergies define and demonstrate institutional performance.

### **Sensemaking of Institutional Effectiveness Models**

In this phase of the sensemaking process, the effectiveness data models are examined from an institutional perspective to reveal the characteristics of actual institutional performance. The strongest KPIs from the clustering process reveal the drivers and constraints of functional performance as well as the consequences of any changes to an indicator. The data models provide hints and clues (indicators) of actual performance, which are then evaluated by the collective intelligence of institutional leaders to make sense of institutional effectiveness and identify opportunities for enhancement and improvement (Birnbaum, 1991). The output of this evaluation process is an action plan for the institution, which distributes the initiative throughout the organization for implementation.

Table 77:    Competing Values Environmental Analysis.

![image](images/C0791007T77.jpg)

![image](images/C0791007T77A.jpg)

### **Institutional Effectiveness Report**

The ultimate determination of the effectiveness of an institution rests with the synthetic thinking of institutional leaders (Freeman, 2017). Synthetic thinking is the act of recombining information into new models to visualize and uncover unrecognized meaning (Breazeale, 2013; Morgan, 1996; Weber, 1996). In comparison, “analysis” is the process of separating or parsing a phenomenon into its constituent parts; “synthesis” is the process of reassembling the data into new models, as demonstrated in this process by the repeated reformulation of a data set into multiple models of performance (Antony & Lott, 2012; Breazeale, 2013; Patel, 2016).

In this application, the leadership uses the functional KPI clusters modeled into the institutional performance to evaluate and understand institutional effectiveness (Harmon et al., 2018; Meier, 2016). If the leadership is distributed across all levels of the institution, the decision-making process engages the collective intelligence of the entire institution into the process of sensemaking (Altaf, 2011; Kroeger & Weber, 2015), which is a critical component of a thorough dynamic sensemaking process.

The authentic models of functional performance created by applying the models of effectiveness provide the information needed (inputs) to make sense of the effectiveness of the institution from the functional data. The KPI clustering strategy uncovers the drivers and constraints, which represent the synergies of institutional performance. Together, these data models describe current institutional performance, which is the starting point for institutional sensemaking.

Collectively, these data models represent institutional performance and the effectiveness of the institution. For each functional section of the report, an initial question helps facilitate the leader’s evaluation of institutional effectiveness. [Table 78](#CH007.html#tab78) provides a tool to map and facilitate the sensemaking process.

The synergy of the participants drives the discussion, which is always enhanced by the diversity in the thinking of the team (Christiansen & Lechman, 2016; Freeman, 2017; Sofo, Colapinto, Sofo, & Ammirato, 2013; Stonebraker & Howard, 2018). The reports are not a substitute for the evaluation of the leaders and their ability to look beyond the data for meaning. Without a diverse and dynamic leadership perspective, these reports provide little more than numbers. It is the integration of the collective intelligence of the leadership that reviews the data and together “look beyond the data,” to make sense of the meaning. This procedure establishes a culture of continuous quality improvement in an educational institution (Brown et al., 1988; Goldman, 1999; Hargreaves, Boyle, & Harris 2014; Leber, 2005; Vinyard et al., 2017).

The ability to facilitate these discussions requires effective planning and collaboration strategies (Meyer, 2015; Middaugh, 2010). Hargrove (1998) presents a comprehensive and useful technique for planning and executing creative collaborations. Strategies for making sense of qualitative data are in Bazeley (2013) and conceptual models for relating data sets in Freeman (2017). The results and conclusions that emerge from sensemaking discussions are recorded and used to develop action plans for institutional enhancement and continuous qualities improvement (Bennis & Bidermann, 2007; Popham, 2011).

Table 78:    Institutional Effectiveness Executive Report.

### **Continuous Qualities Improvement**

The final phase of the institutional effectiveness process is to use the conclusions drawn from these data-based collaborations to enhance or improve institutional effectiveness. One of the names given to this process in organizational management is continuous “quality” improvement. As a performance-based model of institutional effectiveness, the current process seeks to focus attention on the specific attributes and performance of the institution instead of the performance of the humans laboring to cause those systems to perform as intended (Kang, Zhao, Li, & Horst, 2016). This approach moves beyond functional performance as an outcome to conceive of functional performance as a “means to an end,” which is institutional effectiveness. The “qualities” or design of the system produces effectiveness (O’Connor & McDermott, 1997; Martin, 2016) of the functional systems. This approach places the responsibility for institutional effectiveness on the institution instead of the individuals within the institution (Hajisoteriou, Karousiou, & Angelides, 2018). Doing otherwise has been identified as a significant cause of failure in institutional effectiveness initiatives in higher education (Kataria et al., 2013; Thompson, 2004).

### **Action Planning**

The action-planning process occurs in two parts: (1) the identification and extraction of items to consider for improvement initiatives (Hajisoteriou et al., 2018; Stonebraker & Howard, 2018) and (2) the critical evaluation of those enhancements to anticipate the consequences of the performance change strategy (Fooladvand et al., 2015; Middaugh, 2010). [Table 79](#CH007.html#tab79) presents a tool for recording the conclusions or questions that emerge from the leaders’ sensemaking of the Institutional Effectiveness Report.

Table 79:    Action Planning Inputs.

| **Item** || **Supporting Performance Data** |
| --- | --- |
| 1. ||  |
| 2. ||  |
| 3. ||  |
| 4. ||  |
| 5. ||  |
| 6. ||  |
| 7. ||  |

The form asks leaders to identify those ideas for improvement that have emerged from their collective sensemaking of the Institutional Effectiveness Report and records the data from which that idea emerged. From these discussions, no more than seven items are selected for further consideration (Alan, 1994; Linhares et al., 2011; Miller, 1956; Shiffrin & Nosofsky, 1994). The performance data from which each idea emerged is recorded in column 2 to demonstrate and facilitate the evidence-based decision-making process (Stonebraker & Howard, 2018).

From these seven items, three are selected for institutional action or strategic planning. From an institutional change perspective, more than three changes produce a confounding effect on institutional performance, which impacts the ability of the leaders to monitor and demonstrate the effectiveness of the changes in real time (Jones et al., 2016).

[Table 80](#CH007.html#tab80) provides a tool to record the three action items and evaluate them systematically to plan the change initiatives and strategies (Middaugh, 2010).

For each of the three action items selected, the change strategy is recorded, the predicted outcomes, the key performance indicators that monitored to evaluate outcomes, who leads the effort, resources needed to implement the change strategy, and the timeline for completion. The categories of planning may vary by institution and include no more than seven considerations for each change item.

As discussed in the clustering process, the clustered KPI triads identify first-, second-, and third-order effects of changes to a single KPI. The KPI clusters in the Institutional Effectiveness Report provide the information populates the unintended consequences column.

This action planning process creates an institutional change procedure that is data based (Stonebraker & Howard, 2018), systematically evaluated for three levels of consequences (Winnaar, Frempong, & Blignaut, 2015), and charged to project owners (Middaugh, 2010), which facilitates the continuous qualities improvement discussions and integrates the decisions into institutional infrastructure. The systematic assessment of institutional effectiveness allows the conclusions to emerge from the assessment process back into the redesign (changes) of the institutional systems. The results of the changes in all aspects of institutional effectiveness are measured and assessed in the next cycle of assessment, using the same measurements and assessments that identified the change.

Table 80:    Action Planning Process.

## Chapter 8

## **Continuous Quality Improvement**

### **Overview**

A set of hypothetical functional performance data is analyzed for an exercise to demonstrate the process of sensemaking. These data contain the models of performance found in the instruments included in this system, which are consistent with most institutions of higher education. For demonstration, the systems approach is used to compile the indicators into the assessment of institutional effectiveness. To ensure the integrity of the indicators before sensemaking, the power of each of these KPIs was calculated and determined to be sufficient for inclusion as a KPI.

### **Sensemaking**

Sensemaking is the critical and culminating component of the assessment of institutional effectiveness and continuous quality improvement, which is a product of the diverse human intelligence of the institution engaging in the sensemaking process. The sensemaking process requires the collective intuition, judgment, agile thinking, and creativity of the humans in the organization to envision effectiveness and plan innovative improvements (Leber, 2005; Sofo, 2013). However, sensemaking requires the availability of authentic, credible, and trustworthy indicators upon which to base decisions (Khezrimotlagh, Zhu, Cook, & Toloo, 2019).

The purpose of this chapter is to demonstrate the application of indicator modeling to sensemaking (Pîrlog & Balint, 2016). None of these discussions seeks to provide an absolute interpretation of the meaning of the indicators but to demonstrate a search for meaning and effectiveness within the data. The possibilities to explain performance are many, and for clarity, this discussion includes only a few. In practice, the search is for plausibility and all plausible meanings, including those that are anomalous or outliers, are included. The scanning for plausibilities generates thorough, dynamic, and creative sensemaking.

#### ***Sensemaking in Action***

In the system of assessment under discussion, sensemaking for institutional effectiveness employs at least two similar analytic approaches. The first analysis scans the Institutional Effectiveness Report to explore anomalies in intended performance. In this evaluation, intended performance is defined by the MVVs and the KPIs demonstrate actual performance. The sensemaking process “looks beyond the KPIs” to evaluate the effectiveness of institutional performance indicated by the MVVs. From this evaluation process, institutional change strategies emerge for performance improvement (Kulkarni, 2016).

The second analysis evaluates the indicator clusters to confirm causal interrelationships between indicators, which become the drivers and constraints of performance. The drivers and constraints of performance represent the synergies within the functional indicators that produce institutional performance. Each of the triads in the clustering analysis is evaluated to determine the drivers and constraints impacting the effectiveness of each KPI.

In neither of these analytical discussions is the purpose of the search for truth or absolute meaning. Instead, the purpose of the collaborations is to collectively recognize plausible explanations and solutions that are addressed through improvement strategies, tested in practice, assessed with authentic performance assessments, and evaluated for effectiveness (Ioannidis, Fanelli, Dunne, & Goodman, 2015). Sensemaking is a process of formulating questions to investigate further by separating functional performance into its constituent parts (clusters) and reassembling it into a model of institutional effectiveness, which is based upon the relationships within the KPIs (Chan, 2015).

### **The Data**

[Table 81](#CH008.html#tab81) presents the Institutional Effectiveness Report compiled from a hypothetical set of indicators and processed using the indicators modeling procedures discussed in this work. The indicators contain and demonstrate the synergies that exist in most institutions of higher education.

[Table 82](#CH008.html#tab82) presents the definitions of each KPI chosen for this report, based on the characteristics of each component of the system: student acquisition, student experience, and student success.

These KPIs were chosen for this report as they demonstrated the strongest correlations with other indicators for each component of the performance of the system. A power test determined the correlation coefficient that produces enough power for sensemaking. [Table 83](#CH008.html#tab83) presents the results of power tests for three correlations.

The results of the power test indicate that any coefficient larger than plus or minus .200 is producing enough power to include in the analysis. As the sample size and significance level are the same for all calculations, the effect size is an appropriate discriminator of power for all calculations.

The report organizes the performance indicators into a systems approach, which uses the constructs of student acquisition (inputs to the institution), student experience (throughputs to the institution), and student success (outcomes to the institution) to consolidate the performance indicators into meaningful demonstrations of performance. The performance for each of four academic years (AYx) is presented for each performance indicator followed by the current performance target, the calculations of the difference between actual performance and the target, and the trend of current performance (AY4) with the target.

![image](images/C0891008T83.jpg)

The many strong correlations found in the KPI clusters reveal the synergies of institutional performance. There is one cluster that contains only one strong correlation, but most contained more than two strong correlations. One cluster contained a cluster of strong correlations with the same correlation size, although one was negative and the others positive. Overall the report contains powerful KPIs calculated from the indicators collected in the functional assessments, which ensures the validity of the indicators for sensemaking.

### **Sensemaking of the Institutional Effectiveness Report**

The report tells a story of institutional effectiveness on all dimensions of performance, which the sensemaking process seeks to uncover and articulate. The sensemaking process begins with the examination of this report for performance anomalies that deserve further exploration. This phase of sensemaking is exploratory and seeks to identify all possible, plausible performance synergies.

The most concerning performance indicator in this report is the average program completion percentage of 22%, which is an operational indicator. However, the functional interactions or transactions produce that indicator. In this instance, the number of people who start a program divided by the number of people who complete the program not necessarily graduating, separates program completion from student success.

When examined for interrelationships with other indicators, the program completion percentage and the course completion rates covary theoretically and are equivalent. The course completion percentage for major courses is 89%. Projected over eight quarters, the 89% course completion percentage predicts a program completion percentage of 44% for eight semesters. This inconsistency suggests that something is constraining or interfering with program completion other than the course completion.

The average GPA of graduates is 3.80, and the average GPA of those who withdraw from the institution is 1.80. The students who graduate seem to be doing so with a high degree of academic success and the withdrawing students are not failing academically (GPA< 1.0), although their GPA is lower than those who complete the program. The students are completing the major courses, with a 5% failure rate, which is at the level of chance. Why then is the program completion percentage only 22%?

The indicators suggest that the courses are well-designed and delivered but that the total student experience is not maintaining their interest. The component of institutional performance that is responsible for creating a relevant and coherent experience is the curriculum. To confirm this assumption, the program outcomes assessments, which were not a KPI at the institutional level, are evaluated for additional information. Also, evaluating indicator 17, which reports the percentage of students who are either employed in their field after graduation or successfully passed a certification examination for their degree, which is 63% of the students who completed the program, excluding those who pursued further education. This percentage supports the conclusion that the curriculum may not have external validity as only 63% of the students are finding employment in their field of study. These are only some of the possibilities that might emerge from this approach to sensemaking of these data. This first method of sensemaking explores a problem with a single indicator and searches for relationships within the indicators to explain or further understand the performance of that indicator.

The second method of sensemaking from the systems report involves the search to understand the collective performance of the three areas of institutional effectiveness: student acquisition, student experience, or student success. This demonstration evaluates the student acquisition function for sensemaking.

This case poses a “research” question: Are the recruitment and admission processes providing qualified students (inputs) whose needs the institution can serve? This question is a qualitative inquiry about the effectiveness of the admissions processes. The evidence of effectiveness for this function comes from the interactions of several indicators across all three functions.

The average GPA of graduates is 3.80, indicating that the students who are graduating from the program are academically successful. The average GPA of withdrawn students is 1.80, indicating that they are not failing, but having performance difficulties, which is supported by the persistence rate of developmental students at 11%. The average time to withdraw from the program is 43 weeks, indicating that students are resilient and not giving up quickly. The student success number is 14%, which is the percentage of students who meet three conditions: they start a program, graduate, and find a position in the field for which they prepared. The failure rate of the general education courses is 8%, which is slightly higher than the major course failure rate of 5% suggesting that the admitted students may not be as academically prepared as expected but are more adept in their major or professional education. A question that emerges from this data is “are the students adequately prepared to pursue the education the institution provides?”

From an admissions perspective, this may indicate that the admitted students have needs the institution cannot serve, which would suggest a review of the student support services function to determine if some variety of support is missing or if the functions are not performing effectively.

A third variety of sensemaking is to examine a cross-functional process to discover its indicated performance. The processes of the instructional system, which involve student acquisition, experience, and success, is selected for discussion (Dick, Carey, & Carey, 2014). In this instance, the indicators in the student experience section of the report are of prime interest with secondary considerations coming from indicators in the other two areas.

The major course completion percentage averages 92% over five years, indicating that the major courses are delivering the learning. The general education course completion percentage averages 89%, which is consistent with and slightly lower than the major course completion percentages. The major course time to withdrawal average is 2.4 weeks and the general education courses average length of time to withdraw is 2.0 weeks, again demonstrating consistency between those that persist and those that do not persist. From these indicators, it appears that the instructional system is effectively delivering learning. The projected program completion % from this performance factor over eight semesters is 56%. Whatever the problem is with the program completion percentage does not seem to be related to the delivery of the program or the preparation of the students. The question that emerges is “what is the functional problem that is creating the low completion percentages?”

The correlation matrix reveals several strong correlations for program completion percentage, both positive and negative. However, these are correlative and not causative. For instance, the high correlation between student success percentage and completion percentage makes sense since the completion percentage is a factor in the calculation of the student success percentage. Based upon this information, there are no indicators of the cause of the low completion ratios contained within the functional or operational data analyzed in this demonstration. However, due to size limitations, many indicators were not included in these calculations, and other causal relationships are likely.

These discussions have focused on the intended performance of the institution as determined by institutional assumptions including those articulated in the MVVs of the institution. The second phase of sensemaking uses the KPI clusters to uncover the drivers and constraints of actual institutional performance, as differentiated from the intended institutional performance.

[Table 84](#CH008.html#tab84) presents the correlation matrix resulting from a factor analysis of the correlations between these performance indicators in the data set. The correlations between all indicators are calculated to discern the number and strength of indicators. This matrix reveals multiple strong indicators for all but one of the KPIs.

The correlation matrix identifies numerous and strong correlations between the performance indicators. This information is searched for the drivers and constraints of institutional performance to confirm or deny the design assumptions upon which the institutional systems were based (Bedeian, 2015).

The last sensemaking process clusters the KPIs into triads to reveal the drivers (positive correlations) and constraints (negative correlations) of institutional performance. In addition to identifying the strong correlations that define performance, they also identify those passive indicators that do not drive or constrain performance, which may be more valuable information than the drivers.

[Table 85](#CH008.html#tab85) identifies the drivers and constraints calculated for each of the KPIs reported in the Institutional Effectiveness report.

Column 1 contains all performance indicators selected for the institutional report. Column 2 contains the strongest correlation with each indicator and Column 3 contains the second strongest performance indicator for each indicator. Each triad represents the synergies between the performance indicators, which determine institutional performance.

### **Sensemaking of Drivers and Constraints of Effectiveness**

The institutional leaders determine the meaning of these clusters to institutional effectiveness in the sensemaking process. The KPI clusters generate questions to inform and facilitate the sensemaking discussions without specifying solutions or making judgments of effectiveness, which the leaders decide. The following discussions of a sample of the triads are offered to demonstrate the process of constructively examining the causality demonstrated by these calculated drivers of institutional effectiveness. The analyses present some directions to pursue and conclusions that may emerge from these clusters of KPIs. Many other options may be revealed if the sensemaking engages a diverse team of intelligence.

#### ***Student Acquisition***

This triad describes the process of generating leads for institutional programs. In the instance of effectiveness, the consideration is whether the lead process is populating the programs with appropriately prepared students. These data are not a measure of the number of leads each year, but of the influences that drive or constrain that number.

In this case, the number of leads correlates with the percentage of students who apply after inquiring about the institution and its programs. This relationship provides evidence of the connection between the conversion of leads and the number of leads generated, which is expected and obvious. The negative correlation with the average GPA of withdrawing students is a little more revealing. As the GPA of withdrawing students goes down, one might conclude that the reality of the experience did not match the representations made to students before enrollment, which could suggests a lack of effective marketing or orientation.

Apps who start            Leads −0.509            GE course failure rate 0.500

This triad represents a synergy between the number of applicants who start a program after being admitted and the total number of leads, as well as the General Education Course Failure rate. These synergies suggest that as the number of applicants who matriculate into a program increases, the number of leads decreases. In other words, a more significant number of leads do not necessarily produce more matriculations. Popularity and conversion correlate inversely in the admissions process. Likewise, as the number of applicants who matriculate increases, so does the general education course failure rate. These data suggest that a more substantial number of matriculations may produce students who are less academically prepared negatively impacting the effectiveness of the admissions process. The salience of these conclusions makes this a fertile subject for a sensemaking discussion with the collective intelligence of the institution.

Graduates avg GPA            Student success – non-dev students −0.475            NA

The graduate GPA average is an indicator of the effectiveness of the instructional system to support and deliver learning to the students admitted to individual programs of the institution. In many systems, grading is far removed from academic performance and often dismissed as a measure of anything to do with performance. However, the goal of this exercise is to understand the performance of the institution and not the functions. When compared with the program learning outcomes assessment, issues such as grade inflation emerge, as well as the possibility of a grading system that is not aligned with the instructional objectives and unsupportive of student success, which negatively impacts the effectiveness of the grading processes. Like all these indicators, the graduates’ GPA is much more powerful when clustered with other indicators, both plausible and measured.

In this case, there is one strong correlation, and that is a negative correlation with the student success indicator. In other words, the grading system may not support the success of all students, and it rises to the level of detectability with only the least academically prepared students. The appropriateness of this situation is also a factor of the values of the institution and relevant for discussion.

WD students %            Prog avg time to WD 0.731            Graduated percentage −0.680

The percentage of students who withdraw from the institution is a direct measure of the effectiveness of the admissions processes to attract students who benefit from the collective performance of the efforts of the institution. This indicator correlates positively with the average time to withdraw from the program and negatively correlated with the graduation percentage. Both these relationships are logical performance relationships, which suggests that the admissions process is effective.

Prog avg time to WD            WD students % 0.731            Major course failure % 0.698

The average time to withdraw from a program and the percentage of students who withdraw from programs are mathematically related and makes logical sense but may not provide much more information to institutional effectiveness than consistency of measurement. The positive correlation with major course failure percentage and average time to withdraw stimulates powerful sensemaking discussions. This relationship suggests that the success in major courses is a leverage point in reducing the percentage of students who withdraw from programs. There are many plausible causes for program withdrawals some of which are within the influence of the institution and others that are not. This triad suggests that success in major courses may be a driver of student persistence. This group of indicators can generate extensive and meaningful sensemaking discussions that venture into an evaluation of the values of the institution as a part of the search for effectiveness.

#### ***Student Experience***

Major course completion %            Avg LoT to WD from major  
courses −0.584            Graduated percentage 0.582

This triad provides some interesting considerations for the effectiveness of the instructional system. The major course completion percentage correlates negatively with the average length of time to withdraw from major courses, indicating that the longer students persist in a course, the less likely they are to succeed. While this relationship may seem illogical, these relationships suggest that students may be encouraged to persist until there is no hope of success, which could be a positive or negative situation depending upon the values of the institution.

For instance, students who withdraw early in a course have an improved opportunity for success in other major courses due to a decrease in their academic workload. Students who delay withdrawing also experience a negative impact on other courses. The major course completion percentage also correlates positively with the graduation percentage suggesting that a driver in student persistence is the completion of major courses. This information reveals another productive discussion for the sensemaking process.

Major course S/I ratio            WD students % −0.604  
Prog avg time to WD −0.689

This triad informs a discussion of the relationship between class size and persistence. This triad indicates that larger class sizes drive fewer withdrawals from the program and higher program persistence. This relationship is consistent with other data relationships in these KPIs and suggests that the prevailing wisdom about the role of class size in institutional performance deserves examination in the context of this institution.

Avg LoT to WD from major courses            Major course completion % −0.584  
GE course completion % 0.501            GE course stud/inst ratio −0.501  
GE course failure rate 0.501

This triad presents a comprehensive review of the course persistence (avg length of time to withdraw) of students through a comparison between the performance in major courses and the general education courses by the same population. The course persistence is positively correlated with the general education completion percentage and negatively correlated with major course completion percentage suggesting that as the persistence in major courses increases in time, the major course completion percentage increases, the general education course completion decreases, and the general education failure rate goes up. These are not causal, *per se*, but are describing a relationship that deserves discussion.

All indicators negatively correlate with student instructor ratio in general education courses. Again, this suggests that class size is a factor in retaining (institutional perspective) students and their successful completion of programs across the institution. Other plausible indications of these relationships may also emerge from informed discussions of these triads, but the most impactful may be the reexamination of the role of class size in student persistence. Note that the size of the correlations between the three identical secondary indicators suggests a consistency of their relationship with student persistence (student perspective). Other triads not discussed also contain evidence to support the discussions of the role of class size in student persistence.

This example demonstrates how a systematic assessment process reveals and challenges the “traditional wisdom” of institutional performance. In this institution, smaller class sizes correlated negatively with several indicators, which strongly suggests that keeping class sizes small is not improving or enhancing student success. The only way to know is to increase class sizes and see if there is a concomitant rise in the other indicators. If performance improvement is the goal, this is a prudent action item to pursue.

#### ***Student Success Triads***

Program completion percentage            Student success number 0.897  
Avg LoT to complete program −0.781

The program completion percentage and the student success indicator are highly correlated as expected. The average length of time to complete the program is negatively correlated meaning that shorter completion periods yield higher graduation percentages. This information indicates that shorter withdrawal times remove the less successful students from the calculations, which means there is nothing that solves that problem from the institution’s perspective. However, another and equally plausible solution is that the length of the programs is too long for many students. This latter conclusion suggests an opportunity to examine ways to shorten programs to increase student success.

Retaining students is one solution often pursued to improve persistence, but in this case, another possibility for which there is growing evidence externally, students are increasingly only able to pursue shorter programs. The institution has an opportunity to accommodate this interest and preference of the changing student population. The appropriate institutional response may be to create delivery methods that compartmentalize degree programs into a series of shorter programs that combine to earn degrees if that is consistent with the MVVs of the institution. However, this finding calls into question the external validity of traditional degree programs for many institutions.

Student success number graduated percentage 0.897  
Avg LoT to complete program −0.738

This triad provides more evidence of the time factor in student success. The high correlation between student success and graduation percentage is expected. The strong negative correlation between student success and the length of time to complete the program compels discussion among leaders to understand these implications and design initiatives to address the situation. If the institution is seeking to improve performance, this interpretation provides hints and clues of a viable option to consider.

Avg LoT to complete program            Avg GPA of WD students −0.796  
Student success – non-dev students −0.792

This triad provides more compelling evidence to support the need and inform a discussion of program length and student success.

Student success – Dev students            Major course student/instructor ratio −0.793  
WD students % 0.657

This triad provides more evidence of the negative correlations between student-instructor ratio and student success indicators, this time with the developmental or less prepared students who are not developmental. Patterns are emerging from these KPIs to provide credible information for action planning.

### **Conclusions Supported by Sensemaking Process**

As specified in the action planning process, a total of seven items are chosen for further evaluation. From these items that emerge from the sensemaking of the performance triads, three of these are chosen for institutional action items and planned for implementation across the institution.

Based upon the sensemaking of the triads of KPIs conducted in this analysis, [Table 86](#CH008.html#tab86) presents the action items selected for consideration as initiatives and projects to enhance or improve institutional effectiveness. The table articulates the connection between the indicators and improvement efforts.

Table 86:    Action Items.

 
| **Item** || **Supporting Performance Data** |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Confirm admissions representations of expectations || Leads × Apps who start 0.971 × Avg GPA of WD students −0.616 |
| 2. Are inappropriate concessions being made to increase leads (market perception of expectations) || Apps who start × Leads −0.509 × GE course failure rate 0.500 |
| 3. Does the unusually high graduate GPA indicate grade inflation or a misaligned grading process? || Graduates avg GPA × Student success – non-dev students −0.475 |
| 4. Is there sufficient student instruction support for the developmental students? || Graduates avg GPA × Student success – non-dev students −0.475 NA |
| 5. If major course success is improved, does student persistence increase? || Major course completion % × Avg LoT to WD from major courses −0.584 × graduated percentage 0.582 |
| 6. Do larger class sizes increase student success? || Major course student/instructor ratio × WD students % −0.604 × Prog avg time to WD −0.689 |
| 7. Do shorter program lengths increase student success? || Student success number × Graduated percentage 0.897 × Avg LoT to complete program −0.738 |

In addition to the information provided for other assessment functions, the KPI clusters also inform the action planning process to sort the action items into those that are most appropriate for interventions and change strategies. The strongest KPIs identify the leverage points for change in the functional systems, which determine the aspects of performance that provide the greatest change potential. This process provides a framework for the selection of change strategies that have the most significant impact on the performance of the institution (Noumair, & Shani, 2018) and ensures that the action-planning process is evidence-based. Therefore, the KPI clusters provide a systematic process for the selection of change strategies.

[Table 87](#CH008.html#tab87) presents the results of action planning for three of the items selected from the list of seven items. These three action items demonstrate the ability to contribute to the institutional improvement and their presumed potential contribution to the long-term effectiveness of the institution.

Column 1 contains the proposed items for institutional initiatives. Column 2 identifies the proposed solution in general terms, which the departmental staff translate into solutions tailored to the specific needs of their programs. Column 3 suggests possible unintended consequences based on correlations within KPI clusters. Column 4 identifies the KPIs that evaluate the results. Columns 5 and 6 identify the staff involved, and Column 7 identifies a proposed timeline. The action planning process provides a systematic approach to evidence-based institutional strategic planning.

### **Machine Learning Loop**

This sensemaking process generates data-informed, evidence-based, organizational learning for institutional performance improvement efforts. The action planning process uses this learning (data) to plan performance enhancement strategies, which feeds the institutional learning back into the functional re-design of the institution. This information provides an authentic and informed view of institutional effectiveness can be relied upon to evaluate the effectiveness and predict the success of institutional performance improvement efforts (Cabral & Huet, 2014).

#### ***The Systematic Process***

The integrity of this process overall is demonstrated by the strategies and techniques of ML and AI that were adapted as templates to design the components of this assessment system. These theories and techniques structure and organize to the entire process, which produces credible and trustworthy data that can be modeled into visualizations to monitor, demonstrate, and enhance institutional performance. To close the loop on the subject, the methods and techniques of ML and AI presented in [Chapter 1](#CH001.html) are demonstrated throughout the systematic assessment process as follows.

*Authentic data* (Mohammed, Khan, & Bashier, 2017). The data collected in the measurement phase authentically measure the performance of the institutional systems at the individual interaction level.

Table 87:    Action Planning.

![image](images/C0891008T87.jpg)

*Algorithms as measurement strategies* (Domingos, 2015; MacKay, 2003). The measurement data describe the intended performance of the interactions between the user and institutional systems. These algorithms select and frame the performance of the interactions between the machine (institutional system) and the human being (user, constituent) as performance. Institutional performance is measured through the application of multiple theories, frameworks, and models to appropriately define performance (Duque, 2016). Each measurement process collects credible and trustworthy data.

*Internal data*. Only performance data generated within the system is included in the measurement, assessment, modeling, and sensemaking processes. One set of data is used for these processes.

*Acyclical data* (Lerma, 2012; Liu, Yue, & Li, 2011; Wanner, Hajičová, Gerdes, & IOS Press, 2013). The performance measurements used in this system are collected from multiple performance cycles to measure institutional performance without the constraints of periodic cycles.

*Classification* (Anitha & Deisy, 2015; Balyan, McCarthy, & McNamara, 2018; Salles, Rocha, Mourão, Gonçalves, Viegas, & Meira, 2017). The measurement data were summarized into performance categories to assess the performance of the different functions and events. The clustering, data triads, and organizational effectiveness modeling are additional applications of this calculation-based concept.

*Statistical modeling* (Hastie et al., 2009; Yan, Wang, & Lu, 2018). The data summarization, aggregation, and modeling techniques are calculated using statistical methods to analyze and format the data. Specifically averaging, factor modeling, Spearman correlation matrixes, and data clustering are all statistical procedures.

*Dimension reduction and modeling* (Goodfellow, McDaniel, & Papernot, 2018; Saraswati, Nguyen, Hagenbuchner, & Tsoi, 2018; Tangkaratt, Morimoto, & Sugiyama, 2016). The correlation matrix calculated from the raw data as part of the factor analysis reduced the number of variables from 81 variables to 21 variables. The factorial modeling output of the factor analysis is an additional example of this process.

*Association learning* (Piatetsky-Shapiro, 1991). The clustering function identifies models of data that revealed the drivers and constraints of actual system performance.

*Outlier detection* (Angiulli, Ben-Eliyahu-Zohary, & Palopoli, 2014; Ha, Seok, & Lee, 2015; Yan, Wang, & Lu, 2018). The correlation matrix identifies the outliers of performance that explain hidden or repressed characteristics of performance. In the qualitative data analyses, the outliers contain rich and meaningful information to describe system performance in diverse populations.

*Structured prediction* (Du, 2017; Jiuqing, Xu, & Xianhang, 2017). The factor analysis reveals a model of performance using the strongest indicators categorized by the characteristics of performance within each function. In addition, the performance triads correlate the performance of each key performance indicator with others that are directly or indirectly covarying. These are structured processes to predict performance.

#### ***Artificial Neural Network***

The final data (KPIs) were modeled into organizational effectiveness displays that visualized the performance of the institution as a system (institution) of systems (functions). Five different models of institutional effectiveness are employed to differentiate these visualizations and facilitate sensemaking from the data.

The AI visualizations are processed through the conduct of a creative collaboration, which engages the human intelligence to make sense (plausibility) of the information to institutional performance in a constructive and creative method. In this system, the AI does not preclude, replace or substitute for human intelligence; it provides the evidence the human intelligence considers to make sense of institutional performance.

Collectively, these techniques of ML and AI configure the components of this systematic assessment of institutional effectiveness. These techniques generate the processes and procedures, which constitute a system of assessments to describe institutional performance and inform IE decision-making. The information systems produced by this approach ensures the availability of authentic, credible, and trustworthy data with which to evaluate institutional performance and IE.

## **Afterword**

This work offers a systematic method for evaluating the effectiveness of an institution based entirely on data that measure the performance of institutional functions at the point of interaction with the constituents. This set of interactional performance data is used for all data processes and procedures that occur after the initial collection of these data. The data collection, modeling, and visualization processes constitute the beginning (inputs) of the IE assessment process.

With this system, leaders and managers have credible and trustworthy evidence to inform their leadership and management decisions. Improvement in performance becomes a matter of using that information to determine plausible meaning to create performance improvement strategies, which are implemented, measured for effectiveness, and adopted, adapted, or rejected for future performance inclusion. It is this last step, conducted by and with human intelligence, that generates institutional effectiveness.

Institutional effectiveness is a process. There are no “magic bullets,” absolute “truths,” “proven” facts, or other approaches that reduce the performance and effectiveness of large, complex, twenty-first-century institutions to dualistic or linear paradigms. This approach uses objective, mechanized, and automated(able) processes to provide credible and trustworthy information, which is received, responded to, organized, internalized, and used to benefit the institution (Bloom, Engelhart, Furst, Hill, & Krathwohl, 1956). This is a constructive and responsible use of the information provided by the systematic assessment process.

For those who believe that higher education can and must improve performance. The systematic assessment process provides the questions to answer, not the answers themselves. For those who are committed to ensuring that the profession to which their lives have been committed performs at peak levels, a systematic assessment process provides the hints and clues to inform those efforts. In the final analysis, like all human (non-linear), dynamic (interactional), self-organizing (adaptive) systems, it is the actions of human intelligence that determines our success. The technologies are tools to help us. In the case of artificial intelligence, the technology walks alongside us; until the last step. The humanness and enjoyment are in the last step.

Enjoy the last step!

John N. Moye, Ph.D.

January 2019
